**Auxiliary data:**

- Spatial spread used the HDX Flowminder DRC origin-destination matrix from March 2026.[^klein-2026-2-methods-4]
- Province-level ACLED event rates for the 12 months ending 25 July 2026 were used to derive an operating-disruption score.[^klein-2026-2-methods-5]
- The latent-burden anchor from the EpiForecasts BVD-OutbreakSize analysis uses INSP situation reports and Uganda export data.[^klein-2026-2-methods-9]
- The generation interval (mean 15.3 days, SD 9.3 days) is the West African Ebola Zaire serial-interval estimate from the WHO Ebola Response Team (2014).[^klein-2026-2-methods-19]
- The incubation period uses the BDBV mean from the 2007-2008 Uganda outbreak.[^klein-2026-2-methods-20]
- The forward response ramp was chosen against a 540-day anchor based on the 2014-2016 West Africa Ebola Zaire confirmed-case burden.[^klein-2026-2-methods-21]
:::

:::{tab-item} Verheyden and Mudogo (20 Jul 2026)
### Verheyden and Mudogo (20 Jul 2026)<br>Returning to school during Ebola: an exploratory health-zone framework for scenario-based school-system introduction pressure during the September 2026 rentrée in eastern Democratic Republic of the Congo

[Read the analysis](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.08.20.26360976.full-text)

### Overview

This analysis estimates where in eastern DRC an infected school-age child is most likely to reach a school before being recognised and isolated during the September 2026 school return, expressed as expected introduction pressure for each health zone over a one-week window[^verheyden-mudogo-2026b-abstract-1]. It combines recent reported transmission, estimated school-age exposure and attendance, a surveillance/interception probability, and mobility-based importation[^verheyden-mudogo-2026b-abstract-2]. The base scenario gives a combined expected pressure of 10.97 across six provinces, with a sensitivity range of 5.56–20.97[^verheyden-mudogo-2026b-abstract-3], and Bunia, Rwampara and Nizi rank highest[^verheyden-mudogo-2026b-abstract-4]. It is a prioritisation tool for school-linked surveillance and readiness, not a forecast and not an outbreak-size or international-importation estimate.

### Estimate

The analysis does not estimate outbreak size. Its headline quantity is scenario-based expected introduction pressure for infected school-age children reaching schools. At the 11 August cut-off, the base specification gave a combined expected count of 10.97 school-system introductions across the six-province scope during the modelled week.[^verheyden-mudogo-2026b-results-2] The median across 10,000 sensitivity draws was 11.17, with a 2.5th–97.5th percentile sensitivity range of 5.56–20.97.[^verheyden-mudogo-2026b-results-3] This is an expectation under model assumptions, not a forecast that exactly eleven infected pupils will attend school.[^verheyden-mudogo-2026b-results-4]

The pressure was geographically concentrated. Ituri accounted for about 8.47 of the 10.97, North Kivu about 1.39 and Haut-Uélé about 1.03.[^verheyden-mudogo-2026b-results-1] Bunia had the highest base value (λ=2.08; P≥1=87.4%), followed by Rwampara (λ=1.84; 84.1%) and Nizi (λ=1.32; 73.4%)[^verheyden-mudogo-2026b-results-5]. Katwa and Nia Nia each had a modelled probability of at least one introduction of approximately 50%.[^verheyden-mudogo-2026b-results-6] The median imported component was 1.31, with a sensitivity range of 0.51–3.28.[^verheyden-mudogo-2026b-results-7] The main driver of the spread was the pre-recognition school-entry probability: changing baseline q from 0.10 to 0.50 moved the total from approximately 4.39 to 21.95[^verheyden-mudogo-2026b-results-8].

The ranking was more stable than the counts. Bunia, Rwampara, Nizi, Katwa and Nia Nia stayed in the top ten in essentially all simulations.[^verheyden-mudogo-2026b-results-9] The retrospective validation results are as follows.
- Seven-day local pressure gave a pooled ROC-AUC of 0.848, rising to 0.885 with the 14-day measure.[^verheyden-mudogo-2026b-results-10][^verheyden-mudogo-2026b-results-11]
- Adding mobility raised it to 0.952 with seven-day source pressure and 0.953 with fourteen-day pressure.[^verheyden-mudogo-2026b-results-12]
- Of 24 newly affected zones, 13 (54.2%) were in the top 10 and 18 (75.0%) in the top 20 mobility-ranked unaffected zones.[^verheyden-mudogo-2026b-results-13]

### Methods

**Scenario-based health-zone introduction pressure model:** This is an exploratory calculation applied to DRC health zones across the six provinces. It takes the most recent seven-day reported case count and projects it forward as one week of cases[^verheyden-mudogo-2026b-materials-and-methods-5]. Projected cases are multiplied by the assumed 5–17-year case share and expected attendance, then by the probability that an infected child reaches school before recognition, isolation or exclusion[^verheyden-mudogo-2026b-materials-and-methods-6]. The result is expressed as an expected count and converted to a model-implied scenario probability with 1 − exp(−λ)[^verheyden-mudogo-2026b-materials-and-methods-7]. A 14-day weekly-equivalent pressure measure was examined as a robustness check.[^verheyden-mudogo-2026b-materials-and-methods-5]

**Directed mobility-based imported pressure:** This component applies to destination health zones within the same 151-zone study area. Pressure at each origin is distributed to destinations using Flowminder and IOM origin–destination shares and scenario movement fractions[^verheyden-mudogo-2026b-materials-and-methods-8], and the destination's interception adjustment is then applied[^verheyden-mudogo-2026b-materials-and-methods-9]. This separates local from imported pressure and produces a mobility-derived warning signal for zones with no recent local cases.

**Probabilistic sensitivity analysis:** This ran 10,000 Monte Carlo draws, with the 5–17 case share, attendance, baseline interception probability and mobility fractions drawn from split-triangular distributions[^verheyden-mudogo-2026b-materials-and-methods-10]. Week-to-week case-count variability was represented by a Poisson draw.[^verheyden-mudogo-2026b-materials-and-methods-11] It varies inputs to the same model rather than changing the method.

**Retrospective validation of geographic components:** This tests the local-pressure and mobility layers against subsequent reported activity. Only information available at each cut-off was used, with activity assessed over the following seven days.[^verheyden-mudogo-2026b-materials-and-methods-12] The two outcomes were any reported increase in cumulative confirmed cases, and the first confirmed case in a previously unaffected health zone[^verheyden-mudogo-2026b-materials-and-methods-13]. Performance was summarised with ROC-AUC, average precision, top-10 and top-20 capture, and Spearman correlation.[^verheyden-mudogo-2026b-materials-and-methods-14] A secondary exploratory comparison tested three nested specifications[^verheyden-mudogo-2026b-materials-and-methods-15]: local pressure alone, local pressure plus surveillance and access modifiers, and the same with mobility.

**Separate CFR analysis and contextual SitRep synthesis:** CFR was analysed separately using empirical-Bayes shrinkage toward a provincial comparator, treated only as a descriptive signal that did not alter the ranking[^verheyden-mudogo-2026b-materials-and-methods-16]. The ten most recent national situation reports were reviewed qualitatively, with no contextual theme converted into a model weight.[^verheyden-mudogo-2026b-materials-and-methods-17]

### Assumptions

**Pre-recognition school-entry probability (q):** The base value is 0.25, with low and high scenarios of 0.10 and 0.50.[^verheyden-mudogo-2026b-materials-and-methods-25] Health-zone values are adjusted by analyst-specified modifiers for contact follow-up, testing access, health-site density, school-response activity and data quality, clipped between 0.05 and 0.75.[^verheyden-mudogo-2026b-materials-and-methods-26] These weights are not fitted coefficients.[^verheyden-mudogo-2026b-materials-and-methods-27] The authors identify this as the dominant uncertainty: moving baseline q from 0.10 to 0.50 changed the total from about 4.39 to 21.95[^verheyden-mudogo-2026b-results-8].

**School-age exposure inputs:** The population aged 5–17 is set at a national share of 31.96%.[^verheyden-mudogo-2026b-materials-and-methods-28] Attendance is 56.6% at base, with low and high values of 45% and 70%.[^verheyden-mudogo-2026b-materials-and-methods-29] The 5–17 case share is 13.17% and was varied from 10% to 16%.[^verheyden-mudogo-2026b-materials-and-methods-20] These are exposure assumptions rather than observed attendance on 1 September.[^verheyden-mudogo-2026b-materials-and-methods-30]

**One-week continuation and Poisson transformation:** The next school-week case burden is projected from the most recent seven-day case count.[^verheyden-mudogo-2026b-materials-and-methods-5] Converting λ to a probability uses a Poisson assumption that is not tested for clustered household, mobility or school-linked events.[^verheyden-mudogo-2026b-materials-and-methods-31] The authors state it is not an empirically calibrated probability forecast.[^verheyden-mudogo-2026b-materials-and-methods-7]

**Mobility treatment:** Origin–destination entries are normalised across destinations in the study matrix, and origins with no usable row contribute no imported pressure.[^verheyden-mudogo-2026b-materials-and-methods-32] Flowminder and IOM flows are treated as additive, which may double-count some movement; a Flowminder-only sensitivity reduced total λ only from 10.974 to 10.736.[^verheyden-mudogo-2026b-discussion-1] Plausible changes in mobility scaling moved the total much less than q.[^verheyden-mudogo-2026b-results-18]

**CFR and context kept outside λ:** Crude CFRs were not used as transmission drivers.[^verheyden-mudogo-2026b-materials-and-methods-33] No contextual theme was converted into a numerical model weight.[^verheyden-mudogo-2026b-materials-and-methods-34]

**Sensitivity distributions:** Structural assumptions were drawn from split-triangular distributions with the base value as the median.[^verheyden-mudogo-2026b-materials-and-methods-35] The Poisson step is a simple process-variation device that does not establish that introductions are independently Poisson.[^verheyden-mudogo-2026b-materials-and-methods-36]

### Limitations

**Calibration and interpretation of probabilities:** The probabilities are model-implied scenario probabilities rather than calibrated forecasts.[^verheyden-mudogo-2026b-abstract-6] The sensitivity ranges are not calibrated confidence or predictive intervals.[^verheyden-mudogo-2026b-materials-and-methods-37] The result is a pre-rentrée snapshot rather than a direct observation of September attendance.[^verheyden-mudogo-2026b-materials-and-methods-2]

**Extrapolated denominators and borrowed inputs:** School and pupil denominators are extrapolated rather than drawn from a harmonised health-zone school census, and the 5–17 case share comes from a national snapshot rather than health-zone-specific age incidence.[^verheyden-mudogo-2026b-discussion-2] Contact-follow-up and alert-investigation indicators are affected by reporting completeness.[^verheyden-mudogo-2026b-discussion-3]

**Mobility data coverage and interpretation:** Mobility data describe population movement rather than observed pupil or teacher travel, and missing or redacted flows can generate false reassurance.[^verheyden-mudogo-2026b-discussion-4] Vuhovi received a zero mobility score because it was absent from the Flowminder matrix, a coverage failure showing that low modelled imported risk is not proof of no importation risk.[^verheyden-mudogo-2026b-results-19] Mobility may partly capture population size, urbanisation or reporting intensity, and no population, distance or urbanisation comparator model was added.[^verheyden-mudogo-2026b-discussion-5] Data coverage was also uneven, with 96 of 151 health zones flagged as having no health-zone case series.[^verheyden-mudogo-2026b-results-16]

**Scope of the validation:** The retrospective validation assesses reported health-zone activity, not an infected child actually entering a school.[^verheyden-mudogo-2026b-discussion-6] It cannot fully distinguish biological spread from differences in testing access, reporting completeness, insecurity, response attention or data connectivity.[^verheyden-mudogo-2026b-discussion-7] The bootstrap intervals reflect variation across a small number of origins and are not external-validation confidence intervals.[^verheyden-mudogo-2026b-results-20] The nested-specification comparison does not create an independent temporal or spatial holdout.[^verheyden-mudogo-2026b-results-21]

**Reproducibility boundaries:** The package does not fully reconstruct the original rolling-origin score matrices from dated health-zone histories.[^verheyden-mudogo-2026b-materials-and-methods-38] It also does not constitute an independently double-extracted SitRep audit trail.[^verheyden-mudogo-2026b-discussion-8]

**No within-school transmission or school-level modelling:** The study does not model within-school secondary transmission.[^verheyden-mudogo-2026b-discussion-9] The authors state available evidence is sufficient to identify these mechanisms but not to calibrate a defensible school-level transmission model across 151 health zones.[^verheyden-mudogo-2026b-discussion-10] Urban/rural school conditions are not modelled as a separate coefficient because comparable school-level data are not available.[^verheyden-mudogo-2026b-discussion-11]

### Data sources

**Primary outbreak data:** Epidemiological data came from DRC national SitReps and the INRB-UMIE BDBV2026-Data repository, which manually transcribes SitRep indicators.[^verheyden-mudogo-2026b-materials-and-methods-18] The model used recent seven-day reported case incidence as the principal epidemic-pressure signal[^verheyden-mudogo-2026b-materials-and-methods-19]. The age share came from the latest usable standardised SitRep demographic snapshot (455 of 3,454 cases; 13.17%)[^verheyden-mudogo-2026b-materials-and-methods-20]. Health-zone case histories for validation were reconstructed from a reconciled direct-PDF series[^verheyden-mudogo-2026b-materials-and-methods-21]. The primary validation included 1,208 health-zone–origin observations, of which 200 showed activity in the following seven days[^verheyden-mudogo-2026b-results-14]. It also included 930 unaffected candidate observations and 24 first-health-zone events.[^verheyden-mudogo-2026b-results-15] The contextual review used the ten most recent national SitReps (31 July–11 August 2026).[^verheyden-mudogo-2026b-materials-and-methods-3] Of 151 analytical health zones, 96 were flagged as having no health-zone case series and 55 as OK/review.[^verheyden-mudogo-2026b-results-16]

**Auxiliary data:** Health-zone population denominators came from the WorldPop layer in the repository.[^verheyden-mudogo-2026b-materials-and-methods-22] The school-age share (31.96%) and attendance values (base 56.6%) were derived from UNICEF/UN population age bands and UNICEF education indicators.[^verheyden-mudogo-2026b-materials-and-methods-23] Directed mobility came from Flowminder origin–destination data and IOM displacement flows in the repository.[^verheyden-mudogo-2026b-materials-and-methods-24] The Flowminder matrix had nonzero outgoing rows for 132 health zones and the IOM matrix for 43.[^verheyden-mudogo-2026b-results-17] Documentary context included May 2026 Ministry of Education instructions to schools[^verheyden-mudogo-2026b-introduction-2] and older benchmarks such as MICS 2018 indicators cited by UNICEF and Education Cannot Wait[^verheyden-mudogo-2026b-introduction-3]. These contextual items were used for interpretation, not as model inputs.
:::

:::{tab-item} Bouhentala & Kadir (15 Jul 2026)
### Bouhentala & Kadir (15 Jul 2026)<br>Epidemiological Analysis of the 2026 Bundibugyo Virus Disease Outbreak and Rapid Risk Assessment for North Africa and Europe

[Read the analysis](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.07.19.26358411)

### Overview

This analysis assesses the risk of introduction and onward transmission of the 2026 BDBV outbreak into North Africa and Europe, using publicly available aggregate surveillance data synthesized through mid-July 2026. Risk was assessed across four dimensions: introduction likelihood, conditional onward-transmission likelihood, impact, and confidence.[^bouhentala-2026-abstract-1] Introduction likelihood was assessed as very low to low for North Africa and very low for the general European population, with delayed recognition in routine healthcare identified as the principal scenario for limited secondary transmission.[^bouhentala-2026-abstract-2]

### Estimate

This analysis does not produce a quantitative outbreak-size estimate for DRC. It reports observed surveillance counts as descriptive figures rather than model-derived estimates. As of 15 July 2026, the DRC had reported 2,124 laboratory-confirmed cases, 828 deaths among confirmed cases, 390 reported recoveries, and 725 patients hospitalised in isolation, across 46 of 140 health zones in five provinces.[^bouhentala-2026-3-results-4] The headline epidemiological trend finding is a notification-rate ratio: the aggregate rate was 35.9 per day during 2–29 June (970 notifications over 27 days; exact counting interval 33.7–38.3) and 47.4 per day during 1–15 July (664 over 14 days; 43.9–51.2), yielding a July-to-June notification-rate ratio of 1.32 (counting-model 95% interval 1.20–1.46).[^bouhentala-2026-3-results-5] For importation risk, the analysis cites an external ECDC estimate rather than producing its own: ECDC estimated approximately one importation per 23,000–24,000 travellers from the principal outbreak region (90% uncertainty interval one per 13,000–54,000), and under a hypothetical scenario of 100 travellers during 11–25 June, estimated a 0.45% probability of at least one importation (90% uncertainty interval 0.20%–0.85%).[^bouhentala-2026-3-results-6]

### Methods

**Rapid public-health risk assessment using aggregate surveillance data:** This was a rapid public-health analysis of publicly available aggregate surveillance data and official risk assessments, drawing on WHO Disease Outbreak News, WHO/IHR statements and technical guidance, ECDC surveillance and modelling reports, WHO regional offices, national ministry-of-health dashboards, and peer-reviewed primary studies for historical BDBV outbreaks, viral persistence, and candidate countermeasures.[^bouhentala-2026-2-methods-4] Airline sources were used only to establish the existence of plausible routes and not to estimate passenger volumes.[^bouhentala-2026-2-methods-5] This approach covers DRC nationally (with provincial detail for Ituri) as the source population, and North Africa and Europe as destination geographies.

**Interval-specific notification-rate analysis:** Cumulative confirmed DRC counts were indexed by reporting date rather than symptom-onset date, and interval-specific average notification rates were calculated as the change in cumulative confirmed cases divided by the number of days between reports, with exact 95% Poisson counting intervals calculated using the Garwood method.[^bouhentala-2026-2-methods-6] The notification-rate ratio comparing 1–15 July with 2–29 June was calculated on the log scale using SE[log(RR)] = √(1/C₁ + 1/C₀).[^bouhentala-2026-2-methods-7] No reproduction number was estimated because onset-date line-list data were unavailable.[^bouhentala-2026-2-methods-8]

**Structured qualitative risk rating:** Risk was separated into four dimensions — introduction likelihood, conditional onward-transmission likelihood after an importation, impact if detection was delayed, and confidence in the judgement — with categories treated as comparative reference classes rather than calibrated probabilities; boundary ratings such as 'very low to low' were retained when the evidence did not support a more precise classification.[^bouhentala-2026-2-methods-9] The ratings were calibrated against WHO's 6 June assessment, which classified risk as very high in the DRC, high in Uganda and bordering countries, low in the remainder of the WHO African Region, and low globally.[^bouhentala-2026-2-methods-10]

### Assumptions

**Reporting-date indexing and its limitations:** The Poisson counting intervals describe counting variation only and do not incorporate systematic uncertainty from backlogs, under-ascertainment, changing testing, or reporting delays.[^bouhentala-2026-2-methods-13] The observed increase in notification rates cannot be interpreted as a direct measure of biological acceleration, as WHO noted that expanded surveillance, testing, and diagnostic capacity contributed to rising reported counts, and newly reported cases could include backlogs.[^bouhentala-2026-3-results-11]

**Risk ratings as comparative reference classes rather than calibrated probabilities:** Risk categories are comparative reference classes rather than calibrated probabilities.[^bouhentala-2026-2-methods-14] Passenger-volume and confidential readiness data were unavailable[^bouhentala-2026-3-results-12], so country ratings rest on route availability, occupational exposure patterns, and qualitative preparedness indicators rather than quantified traveller volumes.

**Transmission requires direct contact:** A recognised symptomatic case can usually be contained because transmission requires direct contact with blood or other body fluids, contaminated materials, or the body of a deceased case rather than ordinary casual proximity.[^bouhentala-2026-4-discussion-1] This structural assumption underpins the low conditional sustained-transmission rating for North Africa and Europe.

**Border screening ineffectiveness during incubation:** An incubating traveller can cross a border while asymptomatic because people are not considered infectious before symptom onset and the incubation period can extend to 21 days; temperature screening may detect some symptomatic travellers but cannot exclude infection during incubation.[^bouhentala-2026-4-discussion-2]

**Itinerary concealment risk:** A final departure airport may conceal earlier travel to eastern DRC; triage therefore requires a complete 21-day itinerary and exposure history.[^bouhentala-2026-3-results-13]

**Cumulative doubling time not treated as a transmission signal:** Arithmetic cumulative doubling times were treated as descriptive properties of the cumulative curve and not as evidence of changes in transmission.[^bouhentala-2026-2-methods-15]

### Limitations

**Dependence on public aggregate data:** The main limitation is dependence on public aggregate data; reporting dates do not represent symptom onset, and the analysis could not estimate time-varying reproduction numbers, serial intervals, onset-to-isolation delays, household secondary-attack rates, health-zone-specific growth, or intervention effects.[^bouhentala-2026-4-discussion-3]

**Incomplete auxiliary inputs for importation assessment:** Passenger volumes, traveller occupations, deployment rosters, medevac activity, and country-specific laboratory and referral readiness were incomplete; country ratings were therefore comparative judgements rather than calibrated probabilities.[^bouhentala-2026-4-discussion-4]

**Counting intervals understating true uncertainty:** Notification rates remain composite measures of infection, care seeking, alert investigation, specimen transport, laboratory capacity, retrospective death investigation, and reporting; the exact Poisson intervals are therefore deliberately described as counting-model intervals rather than complete epidemiological uncertainty intervals.[^bouhentala-2026-4-discussion-5]

**CFR interpretation caution:** The rise in confirmed-case CFR is compatible with maturation of outcomes among previously active cases, retrospective confirmation of deaths, delayed presentation, preferential detection of severe disease, delayed recovery reporting, and changes in testing or death investigation; it should not be interpreted as a time-resolved measure of clinical virulence.[^bouhentala-2026-3-results-14]

**Unresolved status discrepancy in DRC counts:** The published status totals did not fully reconcile — 828 deaths, 390 recoveries, and 725 hospitalised patients summed to 1,943, leaving 181 of 2,124 confirmed cases outside those categories — and public reports did not define the status of these cases; potential explanations include pending admission or outcome reconciliation, transfers, and reporting lags, but no category could be assigned without line-list data.[^bouhentala-2026-3-results-15]

**Time-stamped analysis:** The analysis was time-stamped and may be superseded by subsequent official reports.[^bouhentala-2026-4-discussion-6]

### Data sources

**Primary outbreak data:** Public surveillance reports from WHO, ECDC, Africa CDC, national ministries of health, and peer-reviewed sources were synthesised through 15–17 July 2026.[^bouhentala-2026-abstract-3] The main DRC trajectory used laboratory-confirmed cases and deaths among confirmed cases; suspected, probable, and confirmed categories were not summed across reports because individuals and deaths can be reclassified as investigations progress.[^bouhentala-2026-2-methods-11] Uganda's official dashboard as of 17 July reported 20 confirmed cases, two confirmed deaths, 18 recoveries, and no current admissions.[^bouhentala-2026-3-results-7] WHO's jurisdictional accounting on 17 July comprised 2,145 confirmed cases globally: 2,124 in the DRC, 20 in Uganda, and one in France, with 830 confirmed-case deaths.[^bouhentala-2026-3-results-8]

**Auxiliary data:** Peer-reviewed primary studies were used for historical BDBV outbreaks, viral persistence, and candidate countermeasures.[^bouhentala-2026-2-methods-12] Airline sources were used only to establish the existence of plausible routes and not to estimate passenger volumes.[^bouhentala-2026-2-methods-5] ECDC Monte Carlo importation summaries (approximately one importation per 23,000–24,000 travellers, with 90% uncertainty interval one per 13,000–54,000) were referenced as separately generated estimates and were not treated as exact reciprocals.[^bouhentala-2026-3-results-9][^bouhentala-2026-3-results-10]
:::

:::{tab-item} Fanelli et al. (5 Jul 2026)
### Fanelli et al. (5 Jul 2026)<br>Importation risk and preparedness priorities across Africa in the 2026 Bundibugyo Ebola outbreak

[Read the analysis](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.07.21.26358330)

### Overview

This analysis estimates the risk of Bundibugyo Ebola virus disease (BVD) importation from the ongoing outbreak in eastern DRC and Uganda to all other African countries, and contextualizes that risk against each country's epidemic readiness. The authors developed an integrated spatial framework combining high-resolution epidemic and population data, air and land mobility, geographical accessibility, and conflict-adjusted travel times, then contextualized importation risk using national readiness indicators.[^fanelli-2026a-abstract-1] Importation risk was highly geographically concentrated, with neighbouring countries accounting for 85% of continental risk; South Sudan, Rwanda, Kenya, Tanzania, Zambia, Ethiopia, Burundi, Angola, South Africa, the Central African Republic, and Nigeria were estimated at high risk, with the largest readiness gaps in South Sudan, Burundi, and the Central African Republic.[^fanelli-2026a-abstract-2]

### Estimate

This analysis does not estimate total outbreak size (case counts). Its primary output is relative importation risk — the estimated probability that an infected traveller from the affected areas reaches each African country — stratified by destination country, mobility pathway, and readiness. Key findings are as follows.

The 11 countries in the high-risk quartile — South Sudan, Rwanda, Kenya, Zambia, Ethiopia, Tanzania, Burundi, Angola, South Africa, Central African Republic, and Nigeria — accounted for 93% of the total estimated importation risk, forming a largely contiguous regional belt surrounding the affected area, with Ethiopia, South Africa, and Nigeria at greater geographic distance (border distance 2, 3, and 3 from the outbreak source, respectively).[^fanelli-2026a-results-1]

Neighbouring countries alone accounted for 85% of the total estimated risk, while countries located two border crossings away contributed an additional 9%.[^fanelli-2026a-results-2]

Ethiopia, South Africa, and Nigeria were the only non-neighbouring countries classified as high risk overall; each ranked substantially higher for air importation risk than land importation risk — Ethiopia ranked first for air but fifth for land, South Africa third and ninth, and Nigeria seventh and eleventh.[^fanelli-2026a-results-3]

Under amplification scenarios, the ranking of high-risk countries remained largely unchanged across the seven scenarios tested, with South Sudan, Rwanda, Kenya, Tanzania, and Zambia remaining among the five countries at highest risk.[^fanelli-2026a-results-4] Amplification in Nairobi and Lusaka produced the largest increase in continental importation (4-fold), and up to 15-fold in southern Africa; amplification in Kampala and Kigali produced a moderate increase (2.5-fold and 2.8-fold, respectively), whereas expansion to Kinshasa, Bujumbura, or Juba produced comparatively limited continental effects.[^fanelli-2026a-results-5]

### Methods

**Integrated multi-layer importation risk model:** The authors developed an integrated analytical framework combining epidemiological and high-resolution spatial population data, commercial air transportation, land mobility, geographical accessibility, conflict-adjusted travel times, and different readiness scores to estimate the risk of Ebola Bundibugyo dissemination across Africa.[^fanelli-2026a-introduction-2] Importation risk for each African country was estimated separately for air and land mobility as the probability that an infected traveller from the affected areas would reach that country, assuming infected cases could travel only during the latent (pre-symptomatic) phase; for land mobility, travel times were incorporated to estimate the destination reached before symptom onset, while air journey times were considered negligible relative to the incubation period.[^fanelli-2026a-methods-4] Risks were weighted by the cumulative attack rate in each affected administrative unit, and air and land importation risks were then combined into an overall relative importation risk per destination country.[^fanelli-2026a-methods-5]

**Air mobility component:** Air mobility was derived from IATA data on origin–destination passenger flows between any pair of commercial airports, accounting for connections at intermediate airports.[^fanelli-2026a-methods-6] Population in each administrative unit was assigned to airports using weights proportional to population size and decreasing with distance to each airport.[^fanelli-2026a-methods-7]

**Land mobility component:** Land mobility was obtained as residual mobility by subtracting air mobility from the Global Transnational Mobility Dataset 2.0 (GTMD2), which provides estimates of overall population movements between countries.[^fanelli-2026a-methods-8] To characterize spatial accessibility and potential for land-based spread within the incubation period, travel-time surfaces were computed using a global motorized-travel friction surface integrating road networks, land cover, rivers, topography, and transport infrastructure, estimated from each affected health district and ADM3 unit to all destination countries, and adjusted for conflict-related disruptions using ACLED data calibrated against Google Maps driving-time estimates.[^fanelli-2026a-methods-9] The share of population in each unit travelling to each destination country was estimated through a gravity model calibrated on observed land mobility patterns.[^fanelli-2026a-methods-10] Sensitivity analyses reduced land mobility by 66% and 90% and varied assumed daily driving time beyond the baseline of 8 hours; these produced only limited changes in country rankings and no changes in the estimated geography of risk.[^fanelli-2026a-discussion-1]

**Readiness contextualization:** National epidemic readiness was assessed using the WHO SPAR (2025), the Global Health Security Index (GHS, 2021), the Infectious Disease Vulnerability Index (IDVI, 2016), and the Epidemic Risk Index (INFORM-WHO, 2020), with indicators on healthcare system capacity and emergency preparedness and response extracted from each framework.[^fanelli-2026a-methods-11] SPAR was selected as the primary readiness metric given its complete coverage of African countries, recent update, and direct relevance to the WHO Framework for Health Emergency Preparedness and Response Capabilities.[^fanelli-2026a-methods-12] GHS indicators were used in sensitivity analyses.[^fanelli-2026a-methods-13]

**Amplification scenarios:** Amplification scenarios were designed to assess potential impact of epidemic expansion beyond the currently affected area into highly connected urban centres, including the capital cities of DRC (Kinshasa) and Uganda (Kampala), and the capitals of neighbouring high-risk countries: Juba, Kigali, Bujumbura, Nairobi, and Lusaka.[^fanelli-2026a-methods-14]

**Reporting-rate adjustment:** To account for geographically heterogeneous reporting rates, the authors used the proportion of alerts investigated among reported alerts in each DRC province, averaged over the two weeks preceding 5 July 2026, relative to 100% reporting in Uganda, and explored alternative values in sensitivity analysis.[^fanelli-2026a-methods-15]

### Assumptions

**Latent-phase travel only:** The analysis assumed that Ebola-infected cases could travel only during their latent (pre-symptomatic) phase, as symptomatic patients would be expected to either remain in the affected country for clinical management or, if transferred internationally, travel under medically supervised and controlled conditions.[^fanelli-2026a-methods-21] This is consistent with the rapid onset of symptoms and progressive clinical deterioration characteristic of Ebola virus disease; however, the authors acknowledge that some individuals with early or mild symptoms may still travel before seeking care, particularly over shorter distances, potentially increasing importation risk to more distant destinations.[^fanelli-2026a-discussion-2]

**Homogeneous travel behaviour:** The analysis assumed homogeneous travel behaviour and exposure risk across travellers, although age, occupation, socioeconomic conditions, purpose of travel, and individual exposure may modify infection risk, as illustrated by the imported case in France involving a medical doctor working in the response.[^fanelli-2026a-discussion-3]

**Reporting rate proxy:** To account for geographically heterogeneous under-ascertainment, the proportion of alerts investigated among reported alerts in each DRC province over the two preceding weeks was used as a proxy for local reporting rates, relative to 100% reporting assumed for Uganda; alternative values were explored in sensitivity analysis.[^fanelli-2026a-methods-15] Geographically heterogeneous under-ascertainment is difficult to quantify, though the results were found to be robust to alternative assumptions.[^fanelli-2026a-discussion-4]

**Land travel duration and daily driving time:** Land-mediated importation risk was estimated assuming an average travel duration of 8 hours per day; alternative travel durations produced no changes in the estimated geography of risk.[^fanelli-2026a-discussion-5]

**Border restrictions excluded:** Border restrictions and closures related to the current outbreak were not modelled because implemented measures were partial, temporary, and largely limited to official points of entry, and available cross-country mobility data suggested no meaningful reduction in cross-border movement occurred during such restrictions.[^fanelli-2026a-methods-22]

**Air travel restricted to commercial passenger flights:** Air-mediated importation risk only considered commercial passenger flights and did not account for humanitarian, military, or charter flights.[^fanelli-2026a-discussion-6]

**Attack rate weighting:** Importation risks were weighted by the cumulative attack rate in each affected administrative unit[^fanelli-2026a-methods-23], meaning areas with higher local epidemic intensity contribute proportionally more to estimated exportation risk.

**SPAR as primary readiness metric:** SPAR was selected as the primary epidemic readiness metric because of its complete coverage of African countries, recent update (2025), and direct relevance to the current WHO Framework for Health Emergency Preparedness and Response Capabilities; GHS indicators were used in sensitivity analyses.[^fanelli-2026a-methods-12]

### Limitations

**Scarcity of land mobility data:** Reliable data on cross-border land mobility in Africa remain scarce; land mobility was therefore estimated from the best available continental dataset, combining multiple mobility sources and deriving land movements as the residual after discounting commercial air travel.[^fanelli-2026a-discussion-7] Sensitivity analyses reducing land mobility by 66% and 90% produced only limited changes in country rankings, supporting robustness, though uncertainty remains.[^fanelli-2026a-discussion-8]

**Restricted analysis to contiguous Africa:** The analysis was restricted to contiguous Africa because comparable data on maritime mobility are not available.[^fanelli-2026a-discussion-9]

**Commercial flights only:** Air-mediated importation risk only considered commercial passenger flights and did not account for humanitarian, military, or charter flights.[^fanelli-2026a-discussion-6]

**Homogeneous travel behaviour and exposure risk:** The assumption of homogeneous travel behaviour and exposure risk across travellers does not capture variation by age, occupation, socioeconomic conditions, purpose of travel, or individual exposure.[^fanelli-2026a-discussion-3]

**Under-ascertainment difficult to quantify:** Geographically heterogeneous under-ascertainment is difficult to quantify; the proportion of investigated alerts was used as a proxy, and results were found to be robust to alternative assumptions.[^fanelli-2026a-discussion-4]

**Border control measures not modelled:** Behavioural adaptations and the impact of border control measures were not explicitly modelled; while some border restrictions were implemented, independent mobility data suggested no measurable change in cross-border movements, and evidence from previous Ebola outbreaks highlights that travel restrictions have limited impact on spatial dissemination while potentially hampering response activities.[^fanelli-2026a-discussion-10]

**Readiness indicators are structural, not operational:** Recent outbreak experience demonstrates that operational preparedness is not solely determined by structural readiness indicators[^fanelli-2026a-discussion-11], and the readiness scores used (SPAR, GHS, IDVI, INFORM) may not fully capture in-the-moment operational capacity built through experience and regional coordination.

### Data sources

**Primary outbreak data:** Cumulative confirmed cases were obtained from the INSP DRC daily situation report number 51 (published 5 July 2026) and the Uganda Ministry of Health update of 5 July 2026.[^fanelli-2026a-methods-1] As of 4 July 2026, the outbreak had reached 1,561 confirmed cases in Ituri, North Kivu, and South Kivu provinces in DRC, and 20 confirmed cases in Uganda.[^fanelli-2026a-introduction-1]

**Auxiliary data — population:** Population data for DRC were obtained from the 2024 health-zone population projections produced by the DRC Information Management Working Group and disseminated by OCHA through the Humanitarian Data Exchange Platform.[^fanelli-2026a-methods-16] For Uganda, population estimates at administrative levels 0–3 were obtained from UNFPA, based on Uganda Bureau of Statistics data.[^fanelli-2026a-methods-17]

**Auxiliary data — air mobility:** Air mobility was derived from IATA data on origin–destination passenger flows between any pair of commercial airports, accounting for connections at intermediate airports.[^fanelli-2026a-methods-6]

**Auxiliary data — land mobility:** Land mobility was obtained from the Global Transnational Mobility Dataset 2.0 (GTMD2), which provides estimates of overall population movements between countries, with air mobility subtracted to yield the land component.[^fanelli-2026a-methods-8]

**Auxiliary data — conflict and travel time:** Travel times were adjusted for conflict-related disruptions using the most recent conflict data at ADM2 level from the Armed Conflict Location & Event Data Project (ACLED), calibrated against Google Maps driving-time estimates.[^fanelli-2026a-methods-18]

**Auxiliary data — readiness indicators:** National readiness was assessed using the WHO SPAR (2025), the GHS Index (2021), the IDVI (2016), and the INFORM-WHO Epidemic Risk Index (2020).[^fanelli-2026a-methods-19]

**Auxiliary data — reporting rates:** Geographically heterogeneous reporting rates were proxied by the proportion of alerts investigated among reported alerts in each DRC province, averaged over the two weeks preceding 5 July 2026.[^fanelli-2026a-methods-20]
:::

:::{tab-item} Gashema & Muvunyi (8 Jun 2026)
### Gashema & Muvunyi (8 Jun 2026)<br>Can stochastic modelling predict cross-border spread of the Bundibugyo Ebola virus outbreak in Africa?

[Read the analysis](https://www.thelancet.com/journals/laninf/article/PIIS1473-3099(26)00352-X/fulltext)

### Overview

A modelling study by Dick Chamla and colleagues uses fitted stochastic SEIRD (susceptible, exposed, infectious, recovered, deceased) ensemble modelling to leverage limited data to predict cross-border outbreak spread and inform regional preparedness.[^gashema-2026-1] The analysis estimates the probability of regional spillover to neighbouring countries, finding a high probability of spread to Uganda (94.2%) and South Sudan (69.3%), while projected risks for Rwanda (8.6%) and Burundi (2.0%) remain comparatively low.[^gashema-2026-2] By linking outbreak dynamics with spillover risk, the analysis moves beyond forecasting disease burden toward informing operational preparedness.[^gashema-2026-3]

### Estimate

The central estimates of regional spillover probability are: Uganda 94.2%, South Sudan 69.3%, Rwanda 8.6%, and Burundi 2.0%.[^gashema-2026-2] These are presented as point estimates of spillover probability rather than case counts or incidence figures. No uncertainty bounds, confidence intervals, or sensitivity ranges around these probabilities are reported in this document. No outbreak size estimate (case count or incidence) is provided.

### Methods

**Fitted stochastic SEIRD ensemble modelling:** The analysis describes a stochastic susceptible, exposed, infectious, recovered, and deceased (SEIRD) ensemble modelling approach developed by Chamla and colleagues, fitted to limited outbreak data to predict cross-border outbreak spread.[^gashema-2026-1] The method produces country-level spillover probability estimates for Uganda, South Sudan, Rwanda, and Burundi. The method is described only at a high level in this commentary; no calibration procedure, specific parameter values, or detailed description of how mobility or epidemiological inputs are incorporated are provided in the document reviewed here.

### Assumptions

**Homogeneous mixing and uniform intervention effectiveness:** The stochastic models assume homogeneous population mixing and uniform intervention effectiveness, potentially overlooking important spatial, health-care, and socioeconomic heterogeneities that shape transmission dynamics.[^gashema-2026-7]

**Data quality and mobility approximations:** Projections are constrained by the quality of early surveillance data and approximated mobility patterns, particularly in low- or limited-resource settings where data gaps are common.[^gashema-2026-6]

**Static behavioural assumptions:** The models are limited in their ability to capture behavioural adaptation and community engagement, including changes in risk perception, health-care-seeking behaviour, intervention uptake, and public trust, all of which can substantially influence outbreak trajectories.[^gashema-2026-8]

**Detection delay:** An estimated 6-week interval between the probable index case and formal outbreak declaration is noted, representing an undetected transmission window that shapes the model's starting conditions.[^gashema-2026-9]

### Limitations

**Incomplete information during evolving outbreaks:** Models developed during unfolding outbreaks inevitably operate under conditions of incomplete information, evolving surveillance systems, and changing response capacities, which can affect their ability to accurately predict transmission dynamics. Accordingly, these models are not intended to provide a precise forecast, but rather to identify reasonable transmission trajectories and inform preparedness decisions.[^gashema-2026-10]

**Homogeneous mixing assumption:** Stochastic models assume homogeneous population mixing and uniform intervention effectiveness, potentially overlooking important spatial, health-care, and socioeconomic heterogeneities that shape transmission dynamics.[^gashema-2026-7]

**Data quality and mobility data gaps:** Projections are further constrained by the quality of early surveillance data and approximated mobility patterns, particularly in low- or limited-resource settings where data gaps are common.[^gashema-2026-6]

**Behavioural dynamics not captured:** The models remain restricted in their ability to capture behavioural adaptation and community engagement, including changes in risk perception, health-care-seeking behaviour, intervention uptake, and public trust, all of which can substantially influence outbreak trajectories.[^gashema-2026-8]

**Scenario-based estimates requiring continuous refinement:** These limitations underscore the importance of interpreting model outputs as scenario-based estimates that require continuous refinement as more comprehensive epidemiological, mobility, and behavioural data become available.[^gashema-2026-11]

### Data sources

The document does not specify the primary outbreak data sources or auxiliary data inputs used in the Chamla and colleagues modelling study in sufficient detail to enumerate them. The study is described as integrating epidemiological, laboratory, and mobility data into real-time risk assessments.[^gashema-2026-5] Projections are noted to be constrained by early surveillance data and approximated mobility patterns, particularly in low- or limited-resource settings where data gaps are common,[^gashema-2026-6] but specific dataset names, time periods covered, or sample sizes are not provided in this commentary.
:::

:::{tab-item} Chamla et al. (8 Jun 2026)
### Chamla et al. (8 Jun 2026)<br>Size of the 2026 Ebola outbreak and risk of cross-border spillover from Bundibugyo virus in Ituri Province, DR Congo, and its implications for preparedness: a recalibrated stochastic modelling study

[Read the analysis](https://www.thelancet.com/journals/laninf/article/PIIS1473-3099(26)00320-8/fulltext)

### Overview

This analysis aims to establish a short-term epidemic trajectory of the 2026 BDBV outbreak and quantify the probability of cross-border spillover into neighbouring countries at elevated importation risk, to guide preparedness priorities. It uses a stochastic SEIRD ensemble model calibrated to laboratory-confirmed DRC cases, anchored at 598 cumulative confirmed cases on 8 June 2026, linked to a daily-hazard spillover model estimating importation probability for Uganda, South Sudan, Rwanda, and Burundi over a 12-week horizon.[^chamla-2026-summary-1][^chamla-2026-summary-2] Under the central scenario (R0 = 1.71), the model projects a median of 990 cumulative confirmed cases by week 12 (24 June 2026; 90% PI 709 to 1,293) and 174 deaths, with cross-border importation probabilities of 94.2% for Uganda, 69.3% for South Sudan, 8.6% for Rwanda, and 2.0% for Burundi.[^chamla-2026-summary-3][^chamla-2026-summary-4]

### Estimate

**Central scenario (R0 = 1.71):** The ensemble projects a median of 648 cumulative confirmed cases by week 10 (8 June 2026; 50% PI 560 to 732), rising to 990 by week 12 (24 June 2026; 50% PI 858 to 1,141 and 90% PI 709 to 1,293), with a projected median of 174 deaths at week 12.[^chamla-2026-results-2] By week 24, the central scenario projects a median of 8,210 cumulative confirmed cases (50% PI 6,636 to 10,287 and 90% PI 5,063 to 13,498) and approximately 1,440 deaths if transmission is merely sustained.[^chamla-2026-results-3]

**Low-transmissibility scenario (R0 = 1.42):** Cumulative confirmed cases reach a median of 870 by week 12 (50% PI 752 to 1,004 and 90% PI 641 to 1,133; 160 deaths) and 3,662 by week 24 (50% PI 2,884 to 4,484 and 90% PI 2,094 to 6,037; 673 deaths).[^chamla-2026-results-4]

**High-transmissibility scenario (R0 = 2.08):** Confirmed cases reach a median of 1,364 (50% PI 1,154 to 1,579 and 90% PI 975 to 1,807; 202 deaths) by week 12, rising to 66,420 (50% PI 56,560 to 78,937 and 90% PI 41,718 to 95,881; 10,644 deaths) by week 24.[^chamla-2026-results-5]

Because the observed confirmed-case trajectory is consistent with the central scenario, the high scenario is not the operative trajectory; it is retained as an upper-bound contingency should control measures lapse.[^chamla-2026-results-6]

**Spillover estimates:** Cross-border importation probabilities over the 12-week horizon are: Uganda 94.2%; South Sudan 69.3%; Rwanda 8.6%; and Burundi 2.0%.[^chamla-2026-summary-4]

### Methods

**Stochastic SEIRD ensemble model:** A discrete-time stochastic SEIRD (susceptible, exposed, infectious, recovered, dead) framework using binomial draws under standard mass-action incidence, applied to the affected health zones in Ituri and North Kivu provinces. No vaccinated compartment is included, as no licensed BDBV-specific vaccine exists.[^chamla-2026-methods-3][^chamla-2026-methods-4] Three transmissibility scenarios are modelled (low R0 = 1.42; central R0 = 1.71; high R0 = 2.08), with response-intensity reductions applied from 15 May 2026 of 20% (low), 25% (central), and 15% (high).[^chamla-2026-methods-5][^chamla-2026-methods-6] Calibration was performed by simulation filtering (approximate rejection sampling, not formal Bayesian inference), accepting replicates if cumulative laboratory-confirmed cases at day 68 fell within ±30% of 598 (range 419 to 777); 400 calibration-passing replicates per scenario were retained.[^chamla-2026-methods-7]

**Daily-hazard spillover model:** Importation risk to Uganda, South Sudan, Rwanda, and Burundi was modelled as a daily Poisson hazard driven by calibrated infectious prevalence, country-specific cross-border mobility, point-of-entry detection sensitivity from WHO Joint External Evaluation assessments, and travel-while-infectious parameters (Uganda 12%; South Sudan 15%; Rwanda and Burundi 8%), with full equations and parameter values given in appendix 3.[^chamla-2026-methods-8] The importation probability for each country is computed as 1 − exp(cumulative 12-week hazard), yielding the probability of at least one importation over the horizon.[^chamla-2026-methods-9] Sensitivity analyses examined travel-while-infectious (5% to 25%), reporting completeness (0.60 to 0.90), and catchment size (250,000 to 500,000).[^chamla-2026-methods-10]

### Assumptions

**Transmissibility (R0):** Three scenarios span published BDBV estimates from the 2007 Uganda and 2012 DR Congo outbreaks: low R0 = 1.42, central R0 = 1.71, and high R0 = 2.08.[^chamla-2026-methods-5][^chamla-2026-methods-12] The confirmed-case trajectory is described as most consistent with the central scenario.[^chamla-2026-summary-8]

**Delay distributions:** The latent period is fixed at 9.7 days and the infectious period at 5.0 days, in line with the broader filovirus literature.[^chamla-2026-methods-13]

**Case-fatality ratio:** Drawn from a uniform distribution (0.15 to 0.25), centred on the observed confirmed-case ratio of approximately 19% (115 of 598 as of 8 June 2026) and consistent with the historical BDBV range.[^chamla-2026-methods-14]

**Reporting fraction:** Set at 1.0 for laboratory-confirmed cases, on the basis that laboratory-confirmed cases are by definition ascertained; no separate suspected-case reporting fraction is assumed.[^chamla-2026-methods-16]

**Response effectiveness:** The model incorporates a transmission reduction factor representing the combined effects of IPC measures, community engagement, safe and dignified burial, and contact tracing, with reduction factors varying by scenario to reflect differing operational effectiveness from the date of outbreak declaration onward.[^chamla-2026-methods-17] The response-intensity factor was applied uniformly from 15 May, though real-world response effectiveness varies by place, time, access, and community acceptance.[^chamla-2026-discussion-1]

**Spatial structure:** The model assumes a homogeneously mixing catchment of 350,000 across seven affected health zones; spatial heterogeneity is not captured, which is stated as a limitation.[^chamla-2026-methods-18]

**Cross-border spillover parameters:** Importation hazard is driven by calibrated infectious prevalence, country-specific cross-border mobility, WHO Joint External Evaluation–derived point-of-entry detection sensitivity, and travel-while-infectious parameters fixed at 12% for Uganda, 15% for South Sudan, and 8% for Rwanda and Burundi. These parameters were not empirically derived for this outbreak and represent the principal sources of uncertainty in spillover projections.[^chamla-2026-methods-2][^chamla-2026-methods-15] Varying travel-while-infectious from 5% to 25% changes Uganda's importation probability by less than 5 percentage points (saturating at the upper end) but alters South Sudan's projected importation probability by approximately 20 percentage points.[^chamla-2026-results-8]

**Calibration procedure:** Simulation filtering (approximate rejection sampling) is used rather than formal Bayesian inference; the ±30% tolerance window was chosen pragmatically given residual data uncertainty.[^chamla-2026-methods-19][^chamla-2026-methods-20]

### Limitations

**Spatial homogeneity and model structure:** Homogeneous mixing oversimplifies spatial heterogeneity across health zones; healthcare-worker transmission was not separately modelled; and response intensity was applied uniformly across the catchment rather than varying by subgeography or population group.[^chamla-2026-discussion-2]

**Single-point calibration anchor:** The model is anchored on a single recent confirmed-case datum (598 on 8 June 2026), making the fit sensitive to that anchor value.[^chamla-2026-discussion-2]

**Informal inference procedure:** Simulation filtering is not formal inference, and informal cross-border mobility estimates are imprecise.[^chamla-2026-discussion-2]

**Response uniformity:** The response-intensity factor was applied uniformly from 15 May, but real-world response effectiveness varies by place, time, access, and community acceptance. Live mobility data and behavioural adaptation were not incorporated.[^chamla-2026-discussion-3]

**Surveillance and reporting dynamics:** Post-declaration case-finding surges probably contributed to rapid apparent growth between 15 and 19 May; the authors addressed this by lowering the reporting fraction and widening the calibration window, but residual confounding between transmission intensity and surveillance intensity remains.[^chamla-2026-discussion-4]

**Early data quality:** Calibration relied on early-phase surveillance counts from the first weekly WHO External Situation Report (data as of 18 May), where the cumulative suspected-case figure of 528 was only partially resolved into 35 laboratory-confirmed cases, and the death count of 132 reflected surveillance signals rather than laboratory-confirmed mortality alone. Misclassification, delayed laboratory throughput, and ongoing active case finding could each shift the calibration anchor in either direction.[^chamla-2026-discussion-5]

**Exploratory rather than definitive projections:** The projections require iterative recalibration as new data emerge, and should be interpreted as an early situational assessment rather than a definitive forecast.[^chamla-2026-discussion-6][^chamla-2026-discussion-7]

**Absence of sex and gender disaggregation:** Individual-level sex and gender data were not available in the aggregate surveillance reports, making disaggregated analysis by sex or gender impossible.[^chamla-2026-discussion-8]

### Data sources

**Primary outbreak data:** Case data were taken from WHO AFRO External Situation Reports and the WHO AFRO internal tracker through to 8 June 2026. The laboratory-confirmed case series used as the primary calibration metric rose from 33 cases on 18 May 2026 to 105 on 24 May 2026, and 598 on 8 June 2026.[^chamla-2026-methods-11] Uganda reported seven confirmed cases (three imported; four locally acquired, including three healthcare workers) as of 24 May 2026.[^chamla-2026-summary-7] As of 4 June 2026, Uganda reported 19 confirmed cases, including five healthcare-worker infections and two deaths.[^chamla-2026-results-7]

**Auxiliary data:** Country-specific point-of-entry detection sensitivity was drawn from WHO Joint External Evaluation assessments.[^chamla-2026-methods-2] The three R0 values (1.42, 1.71, 2.08) were chosen to span the range of published BDBV estimates from the 2007 Uganda and 2012 DR Congo outbreaks.[^chamla-2026-methods-5][^chamla-2026-methods-12] The latent period (9.7 days) and infectious period (5.0 days) were taken from the broader filovirus literature.[^chamla-2026-methods-13] The case-fatality ratio was drawn from a uniform distribution (0.15 to 0.25), centred on the confirmed-case ratio observed in the current outbreak (approximately 19%; 115 of 598 as of 8 June 2026) and consistent with the historical BDBV range.[^chamla-2026-methods-14] Travel-while-infectious and cross-border mobility parameters were not empirically derived for this outbreak and represent the principal sources of uncertainty in spillover projections.[^chamla-2026-methods-15]
:::

:::{tab-item} Walekhwa et al. (5 Jun 2026)
### Walekhwa et al. (5 Jun 2026)<br>Network-based modelling of Bundibugyo Ebola virus disease importation and spread in Uganda using Displacement Tracking Matrix flow data

[Read the analysis](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.06.22.26356210)

### Overview

This analysis aims to quantify district-level importation and spread risk of the 2026 Bundibugyo virus disease outbreak in Uganda, originating from the ongoing DRC epidemic, in order to inform where surveillance and response resources should be concentrated. It constructs a data-driven directed weighted mobility network from IOM Displacement Tracking Matrix flows (11,245 observed movements, 15–24 May 2026) and the 2024 Uganda census, then runs a stochastic metapopulation SEIR model over 90 days across 135 Ugandan districts and two DRC provinces.[^walekhwa-2026-abstract-1] Under the containment scenario, the model projects a median of 22 cumulative cases (95% CrI: 22–24) and 2 deaths concentrated entirely in Kampala over 90 days, with all other 134 districts projecting a median of 0 cases, and no statistically significant reduction from adding non-pharmaceutical interventions at any compliance level.[^walekhwa-2026-abstract-2]

### Estimate

Under the containment scenario (effective R = 0.35), the model projected a median of 22 cumulative cases (95% CrI: 22–24) and 2 deaths (95% CrI: 2–4) over 90 days, all concentrated in Kampala, with 3 HCW infections and 0 HCW deaths; 95% credible intervals overlapped substantially across compliance scenarios, indicating no statistically significant difference between them.[^walekhwa-2026-3-results-1]

Kampala was the only district with a non-zero median projection, bearing a predicted burden of 22 cases (95% CrI: 22–24) and a 100% probability of a local outbreak (>10 cases); all other 134 districts had a median of 0 predicted cases, with upper credible intervals extending to 1 in only a small fraction of simulations.[^walekhwa-2026-3-results-2]

The three compliance scenarios corresponded to the following parameterisations: containment (effective R₀ = 0.35, mobility scale 0.05), moderate compliance (R₀ = 0.40, mobility scale 0.08), and low compliance (R₀ = 0.45, mobility scale 0.12).[^walekhwa-2026-2-methods-12] Across all three scenarios, the median total case count remained 22, with no scenario producing a statistically distinct outcome.

### Methods

**Directed weighted mobility network:** Both undirected and directed weighted networks were built from DTM flow data using the igraph package in R; for the directed network, original flow directions were preserved to allow separate computation of in-strength (import risk) and out-strength (export risk) for each node, covering all 135 Ugandan districts and two DRC provinces.[^walekhwa-2026-2-methods-4] To impute 12-month movement estimates from the 10-day observed flows, observed weights were multiplied by 36.5, and uncertainty from the limited sampling window was assessed via bootstrap resampling with 1,000 replicates.[^walekhwa-2026-2-methods-5]

**Stochastic metapopulation SEIR model:** A discrete-time stochastic SEIR model is maintained at the node (district) level, with five compartments — Susceptible, Exposed, Infectious, Recovered, Dead — a latent period of 7.5 days, and an infectious period of 8.5 days.[^walekhwa-2026-2-methods-6] Pre-symptomatic transmission is modelled by setting the relative infectiousness of exposed individuals to 0.5 compared to infectious individuals, based on estimates from early EVD outbreaks and the empirical observation that six secondary cases in Kampala tested positive while under quarantine.[^walekhwa-2026-2-methods-7] The baseline transmission rate was derived from R₀ = 2.76, calibrated to the early growth of the outbreak (16 cases in 21 days).[^walekhwa-2026-2-methods-8] DRC provinces were modelled as explicit nodes with their own SEIR dynamics, recalibrated to actual reported data as of May 25, 2026 (381 confirmed cases, 2,987 exposed contacts, and 64 deaths).[^walekhwa-2026-2-methods-9] The model was run for three NPI compliance scenarios (20%, 40%, 60%) with 1,000 stochastic simulations each. The 7-1-7 agile response was explicitly modelled with dynamic contact tracing coverage (40% → 70% → 85%).[^walekhwa-2026-abstract-5]

**Sobol global sensitivity analysis:** To quantify the influence of parameter uncertainty on total cumulative case count, a global sensitivity analysis using the Sobol method was performed, with first-order indices measuring variance explained by each parameter alone and total-order indices measuring variance including all interactions.[^walekhwa-2026-2-methods-10] The design used quasi-random Sobol sequences with N = 500 samples (6,500 model evaluations total), with each parameter set averaged over 3 stochastic replicates to reduce noise, and Sobol indices estimated with 200 bootstrap replicates for 95% confidence intervals.[^walekhwa-2026-2-methods-11]

### Assumptions

**Mobility data representativeness and scaling:** The DTM flow data covering a 10-day period are assumed to be representative of average daily movement patterns after scaling by 36.5 to impute annual estimates.[^walekhwa-2026-2-methods-17] Movement is assumed independent each day (no memory), with only exposed and infectious individuals moving, and the probability of moving identical for both compartments.[^walekhwa-2026-2-methods-18] The analysis acknowledges that movements through unofficial crossings or after the outbreak escalated may not be captured, a limitation common to mobility-based epidemiological studies.[^walekhwa-2026-4-discussion-1]

**Transmission parameters:** The latent period is fixed at 7.5 days and the infectious period at 8.5 days.[^walekhwa-2026-2-methods-19] The baseline transmission rate is derived from R₀ = 2.76, calibrated to the early growth of the outbreak (16 cases in 21 days).[^walekhwa-2026-2-methods-8] Pre-symptomatic infectiousness is set at 0.5 relative to fully infectious individuals, based on estimates from early EVD outbreaks and the observation that six secondary cases tested positive while under quarantine.[^walekhwa-2026-2-methods-7]

**Case fatality and healthcare worker attack rates:** The CFR (2/20 = 0.10) and HCW attack rate (3/20 = 0.15) are assumed constant throughout the simulation.[^walekhwa-2026-2-methods-20] The DRC CFR is fixed at 64/381 = 0.168, based on reported outbreak data.[^walekhwa-2026-2-methods-21]

**Detection and isolation:** A detection delay of 3 days is assumed to apply uniformly to all infected individuals, and quarantine after detection is assumed perfect — isolated individuals no longer transmit.[^walekhwa-2026-2-methods-22] Reported incidence uses a detection probability of p_detect = 1.0, assuming all symptomatic cases are detected.[^walekhwa-2026-2-methods-23]

**DRC epidemic independence:** The DRC epidemic curve is assumed to be independent of Uganda's interventions and to drive importations solely through the movement matrix.[^walekhwa-2026-2-methods-24] DRC response parameters reflect a moderately delayed response: detection delay 7 days (based on the 2018–2020 DRC outbreak), notification delay 3 days, response delay 5 days, contact tracing coverage 30%, detection probability 0.7, and no behavioural response.[^walekhwa-2026-2-methods-25]

**NPI compliance and homogeneity:** Compliance with NPIs is assumed constant over the 90 days and homogeneous across districts.[^walekhwa-2026-2-methods-26]

**Geographic scope of importations:** No importations from countries other than DRC are considered.[^walekhwa-2026-2-methods-3]

**Superspreading:** New infections are drawn from a negative binomial distribution to capture superspreading, with dispersion parameter k_disp = 0.3.[^walekhwa-2026-2-methods-27]

**Homogeneous mixing within districts:** The homogeneous mixing assumption within districts likely overestimates transmission in low-density areas and underestimates it in high-density slums.[^walekhwa-2026-4-discussion-2]

### Limitations

**Mobility data coverage and temporal representativeness:** The DTM data cover only a 10-day window and eight border crossing points; movements through unofficial crossings or after the outbreak escalated may not be captured. While annual estimates are imputed using time-dependent decay, real mobility patterns can change unpredictably.[^walekhwa-2026-4-discussion-3]

**Homogeneous mixing within districts:** The homogeneous mixing assumption within districts likely overestimates transmission in low-density areas and underestimates it in high-density slums.[^walekhwa-2026-4-discussion-2]

**Unstable Sobol confidence intervals from small outbreak size:** The Sobol confidence intervals for some parameters remained wide because the small outbreak size (median 22 cases) produces low output variance, making precise quantitative ranking unstable; the authors therefore interpret the Sobol results qualitatively as ranking parameter importance rather than providing precise quantitative estimates.[^walekhwa-2026-4-discussion-4]

**Hospital and funeral transmission not explicitly modelled:** Hospital and funeral transmission are not explicitly modelled, a limitation given that Legrand et al. (2007) identified nosocomial transmission and unsafe burials as major amplifiers in Ebola outbreaks.[^walekhwa-2026-4-discussion-5]

**No importations from countries other than DRC:** Importations from neighbouring countries other than DRC were not considered, though the 79.9% importation risk estimated by Chalma et al. (2026) focused specifically on DRC-origin introductions.[^walekhwa-2026-4-discussion-6]

### Data sources

**Primary outbreak data:** The Uganda Ministry of Health EVD situation report as of June 5, 2026 provided initial outbreak conditions: 15 cumulative cases (plus 1 probable), 2 deaths, 5 recoveries, 720 cumulative contacts listed (455 active under follow-up), with all initial cases located in Kampala under institutional quarantine.[^walekhwa-2026-2-methods-13] DRC model initialisation used actual reported data as of May 25, 2026 — 381 confirmed cases, 2,987 exposed contacts, and 64 deaths — with Nord-Kivu initialised at 4,000 exposed and 400 infectious individuals, and Ituri at 500 exposed and 50 infectious.[^walekhwa-2026-2-methods-14]

**Auxiliary data — mobility:** The IOM DTM Flow Monitoring report covering 15–24 May 2026 summarised cross-border movements from DRC into Uganda and internal movements among Ugandan districts; 11,245 total observed individuals were distributed across eight Flow Monitoring Points: Ntoroko Main (28%), Mpondwe (18%), Busunga (18%), Bunagana (12%), Cyanika (8%), Goli (8%), Vurra (3%), and Busanza (3%).[^walekhwa-2026-2-methods-15]

**Auxiliary data — demographic:** The official 2024 Uganda Population and Housing Census final report (UBOS, December 2024) provided the de facto population for each of the 135 districts, totalling 45,905,417.[^walekhwa-2026-2-methods-16]

**Auxiliary data — parameters from prior outbreaks and literature:** Previous BVD outbreaks in Uganda (2007–08; 131 confirmed cases, 42 deaths) and DRC (2012; 38 confirmed cases, 13 deaths) informed background on transmissibility and CFR, with R₀ estimates ranging from 1.2 to 2.6.[^walekhwa-2026-research-in-context-1] Bangelesa et al. contributed Flowminder-based mobility intensity index data for Ituri Province (March 2025–March 2026), covering all 340 DRC health zones; Ituri had the second highest average MII nationally (25.3), with epicentre zones Rwampara (68.6), Bunia (62.6), and Mongbwalu (49.9) all exceeding the 95th national percentile.[^walekhwa-2026-research-in-context-2]
:::

:::{tab-item} Kinoshita, Suzuki, & Yoneoka (4 Jun 2026)
### Kinoshita, Suzuki, & Yoneoka (4 Jun 2026)<br>Early assessment of potential airline-mediated importation risk during the 2026 DRC-Uganda Bundibugyo virus disease outbreak

[Read the analysis](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.06.01.26354569)

### Overview

This analysis projects potential airline-mediated importation risk during the 2026 BVD outbreak in DRC and Uganda, using a contemporary airline network dataset and an importation hazard parameter calibrated from historical Ebola outbreak data.[^kinoshita-2026-abstract-1] The approach converts airline passenger-flow connectivity into country-level arrival probabilities over a defined time horizon. The main finding identifies major international hub countries — Belgium, France, South Africa, Kenya, and the United Arab Emirates — as the highest-probability destinations for potential importation until the end of June 2026.[^kinoshita-2026-abstract-2]

### Estimate

This analysis produces importation probability estimates rather than outbreak size estimates. Airport-level arrival probabilities, interpreted as the probability of at least one airline-mediated importation until the end of June 2026 (t = 44), ranged from 0.247 to 0.937, with a mean of 0.431.[^kinoshita-2026-airline-flow-based-network-and-effective-distance-for-10] Higher arrival probabilities were concentrated among airports with shorter effective distances from FIH and stronger connectivity through major transit hubs: for example, 0.889 at Brussels Airport, 0.888 at Paris Charles de Gaulle Airport, and 0.871 at Johannesburg O.R. Tambo International Airport.[^kinoshita-2026-airline-flow-based-network-and-effective-distance-for-11] The highest country-level arrival probabilities were observed for Belgium, France, South Africa, Kenya, and the United Arab Emirates, with the top-ranked countries all exceeding 0.77; in sensitivity analyses the probabilities for these countries ranged from 0.38 to 0.98.[^kinoshita-2026-airline-flow-based-network-and-effective-distance-for-12] When the starting airport is changed to EBB (Uganda) in sensitivity analysis, the mean (standard deviation) of arrival probability ranged from 0.170 (0.049) to 0.616 (0.099).[^kinoshita-2026-airline-flow-based-network-and-effective-distance-for-13]

### Methods

**Effective-distance airline importation model:** Potential airline-mediated importation risk was projected using a hazard-based model informed by contemporary airline transportation networks and a hazard parameter calibrated from the 2013–2016 West African Ebola virus disease epidemic, one of the few EBOD outbreaks with documented long-distance airline-mediated international exportations.[^kinoshita-2026-abstract-4] A directed, weighted air-traffic network was constructed using airport-level origin-destination passenger-flow data from OAG for May 2024, comprising 11,264 airports, 569,741 routes, and 329,326,078 passengers.[^kinoshita-2026-airline-flow-based-network-and-effective-distance-for-4] The shortest-path effective distance from FIH to each destination airport was computed using Dijkstra's algorithm on the directed network.[^kinoshita-2026-airline-flow-based-network-and-effective-distance-for-5] The arrival hazard at each destination airport was modelled as λⱼ = θ/Dⱼ (where θ = 0.19, the primary hazard parameter), and the arrival probability within t days was computed as pⱼ(t) = 1 − exp(−tλⱼ).[^kinoshita-2026-airline-flow-based-network-and-effective-distance-for-6] Country-level arrival risk was summarized as the maximum airport-level probability within each country.[^kinoshita-2026-airline-flow-based-network-and-effective-distance-for-7] Sensitivity analyses tested θ = {0.06, 0.32} based on the 95% confidence interval of the historical parameter estimate.[^kinoshita-2026-airline-flow-based-network-and-effective-distance-for-8] An additional sensitivity analysis varied the effective-distance scaling parameter δ using epidemic growth rates r = {0.034, 0.05, 0.1} estimated as of 17 May 2026.[^kinoshita-2026-airline-flow-based-network-and-effective-distance-for-9]

### Assumptions

**Transferability of historical hazard parameter:** The model assumes that the relationship between effective distance and EVD importation observed during the 2013–2016 West African epidemic is transferable to the current BVD outbreak. The estimated effective distance distribution was reported as very similar to that of the previous study, which the authors cite as supporting use of the same θ value on a comparable hazard scale.[^kinoshita-2026-airline-flow-based-network-and-effective-distance-for-16]

**Effective-distance scaling:** In the main analysis, the distance-scaling parameter δ is set to 1, assuming each additional travel step contributes one unit of distance (the Brockmann-Helbing scale), which has been empirically shown to predict global spread patterns of infectious diseases.[^kinoshita-2026-airline-flow-based-network-and-effective-distance-for-17] An alternative δ formulation incorporating the epidemic growth rate and outbound passenger flow from FIH is tested in sensitivity analysis, but the authors note this increases uncertainty.[^kinoshita-2026-airline-flow-based-network-and-effective-distance-for-18]

**Hazard parameter sensitivity:** The primary hazard value θ = 0.19 is varied to {0.06, 0.32} (the 95% CI bounds of the historical estimate) in sensitivity analyses. These sensitivity runs change absolute arrival probabilities but broadly preserve the overall geographic pattern, because effective distance determines the spatial pattern while the hazard parameter mainly scales the probabilities.[^kinoshita-2026-airline-flow-based-network-and-effective-distance-for-8][^kinoshita-2026-airline-flow-based-network-and-effective-distance-for-19]

**Static airline network:** The estimates rely on 2024 airline network data and therefore may not capture subsequent changes in routes, passenger volumes, airspace restrictions, or travel advisories, including those affecting routes through Russia and the Middle East.[^kinoshita-2026-airline-flow-based-network-and-effective-distance-for-20]

**No post-PHEIC interventions:** The projections do not explicitly account for post-PHEIC interventions, enhanced screening, or behavioural changes.[^kinoshita-2026-airline-flow-based-network-and-effective-distance-for-21]

**Source airport scope:** The analysis models global air travel originating from FIH as the primary source, treating EBB only in sensitivity analysis, and does not explicitly account for land-based spread within DRC or cross-border movements to neighbouring countries.[^kinoshita-2026-airline-flow-based-network-and-effective-distance-for-3]

**Importation versus transmission:** Estimated arrival probability is interpreted as importation risk rather than the risk of local transmission after arrival, on the basis that EBOD requires direct contact with infectious body fluids or contaminated materials, and that timely detection, isolation, contact tracing, and infection prevention and control can reduce onward transmission.[^kinoshita-2026-airline-flow-based-network-and-effective-distance-for-22]

### Limitations

**Reliance on a borrowed, cross-pathogen hazard parameter:** Because no airline-mediated importation had been reported as of 28 May 2026, the hazard parameter was estimated from the 2013–2016 West African EVD epidemic rather than from current BVD data, and the model assumes this historical relationship is transferable to the present outbreak.[^kinoshita-2026-airline-flow-based-network-and-effective-distance-for-23]

**Static airline data and absence of intervention effects:** The projections rely on May 2024 airline network data and therefore may not capture subsequent route changes, passenger volume shifts, airspace restrictions, travel advisories, or the effect of post-PHEIC interventions, enhanced screening, and behavioural changes.[^kinoshita-2026-airline-flow-based-network-and-effective-distance-for-24]

**Geographic scope limited to air travel from two airports:** The analysis focuses on global air travel originating from FIH, with EBB considered only in sensitivity analysis, and does not explicitly account for land-based spread within DRC or cross-border movements to neighbouring countries.[^kinoshita-2026-airline-flow-based-network-and-effective-distance-for-3]

**Importation risk only, not local transmission risk:** Estimated arrival probability should be interpreted as importation risk rather than the risk of local transmission after arrival.[^kinoshita-2026-airline-flow-based-network-and-effective-distance-for-25] Countries with high-probability airport gateways should therefore be interpreted as priorities for awareness and preparedness, rather than as locations where local outbreaks are expected.[^kinoshita-2026-airline-flow-based-network-and-effective-distance-for-26]

**Increased uncertainty in alternative δ formulation:** The sensitivity analysis δ formulation incorporates the epidemic growth rate and outbound passenger flow, but the authors note that while this adds information about the current epidemic situation, it also increases uncertainty.[^kinoshita-2026-airline-flow-based-network-and-effective-distance-for-18]

### Data sources
