**Primary outbreak data:** Epidemiological context was drawn from publicly available reports: as of 4 June 2026, DRC had reported 363 confirmed cases including 62 deaths, and Uganda had reported 15 confirmed cases including one death.[^kinoshita-2026-abstract-3] Because no airline-mediated importation had been reported as of 28 May 2026, no exported-case numerator was available to calibrate the current-outbreak hazard.[^kinoshita-2026-airline-flow-based-network-and-effective-distance-for-14]

**Auxiliary data:** Airline passenger-flow data came from OAG (a global travel data provider listed in the IATA Strategic Partners Directory) for May 2024, covering 11,264 airports, 569,741 routes, and 329,326,078 passengers.[^kinoshita-2026-airline-flow-based-network-and-effective-distance-for-4] The importation hazard parameter (θ = 0.19) was calibrated from the 2013–2016 West African EVD epidemic and applied here as the primary value.[^kinoshita-2026-abstract-4][^kinoshita-2026-airline-flow-based-network-and-effective-distance-for-15] Epidemic growth rate estimates (r = {0.034, 0.05, 0.1}) used in sensitivity analyses were taken as of 17 May 2026.[^kinoshita-2026-airline-flow-based-network-and-effective-distance-for-9]
:::

:::{tab-item} Okingo, Iroungou, & Valdano (4 Jun 2026)
### Okingo, Iroungou, & Valdano (4 Jun 2026)<br>Introduction and sustained-transmission risk across DRC health zones during the Bundibugyo virus disease outbreak

[Read the analysis](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.06.11.26355237)

### Overview

This analysis estimates two related but distinct quantities for the ongoing Bundibugyo virus disease outbreak in DRC: the risk that a health zone will *receive* an introduced case (introduction risk), and the risk that, once introduced, a case would generate ongoing local spread (sustained-transmission potential). The approach applies a previously published method (Ref. 2) combined with population and mobility data to rank all DRC health zones on both dimensions under a range of transmissibility and contact-pattern assumptions. The main finding is that introduction risk was highest in HZs close to the reported foci, specifically in Ituri, Nord-Kivu, and Haut-Uele, consistent with expected mobility patterns, with non-negligible risk of introduction also appearing in distant urban centers, including the Kinshasa area[^okingo-2026-body-1], while high sustained-transmission potential was distributed across DRC, including Kinshasa, Kasaï-Oriental, Kongo-Central, Tshopo, Nord-Kivu, Haut-Katanga, Sud-Kivu, Kasaï, and Kasaï-Central, with several areas far from the current outbreak[^okingo-2026-body-2].

### Estimate

This analysis does not produce an outbreak size estimate; it produces risk rankings rather than case counts. The headline finding is a ranking-based one: introduction risk was highest in HZs close to the reported foci, specifically in Ituri, Nord-Kivu, and Haut-Uele, consistent with expected mobility patterns[^okingo-2026-body-8], while sustained-transmission potential was more widely distributed, and crossing the two rankings identified eight HZs that combined high introduction risk and high sustained-transmission potential: Binza Ozone and Binza Meteo in Kinshasa; Karisimbi in Nord-Kivu; Kadutu and Ibanda in Sud-Kivu; and Makiso-Kisangani, Kabondo, and Mangobo in Tshopo[^okingo-2026-body-4]. The authors note that unlike risk estimates, risk rankings were robust across scenarios and assumptions and can therefore inform action despite limited knowledge of transmissibility and contact patterns[^okingo-2026-body-9], indicating that point risk-level estimates vary with scenario assumptions but the relative ordering does not.

### Methods

**Sustained-transmission potential model (extension of Ref. 2):** The analysis used the method developed in Ref. 2 to estimate, for each DRC health zone, the potential for an introduced case to generate sustained transmission[^okingo-2026-body-5]. It was parameterized using available population and mobility data to parameterize spatial transmission, testing a broad range of assumptions on disease transmissibility and on how local and between-community contact patterns can be inferred from these data[^okingo-2026-body-6]. This method is applied nationally across DRC health zones, producing a ranking of sustained-transmission potential.

**Introduction-risk ranking from mobility data:** Separately, the analysis ranked HZs by sustained-transmission potential and compared them with introduction-risk ranks estimated from mobility data[^okingo-2026-body-7], using flowminder.org mobility data and worldpop.org population data. This produces a national health-zone-level ranking of introduction risk, which is then crossed with the sustained-transmission ranking to identify health zones high on both dimensions.

### Assumptions

**Transmissibility and contact-pattern scenarios:** The model tests a broad range of assumptions on disease transmissibility and on how local and between-community contact patterns can be inferred from population and mobility data[^okingo-2026-body-6], rather than fixing a single value; this is a scenario sweep rather than a single point estimate.

**Ranking robustness versus level uncertainty:** A key structural claim is that unlike risk estimates, risk rankings were robust across scenarios and assumptions and can therefore inform action despite limited knowledge of transmissibility and contact patterns[^okingo-2026-body-9]. This implies the underlying point estimates of risk level are sensitive to the untested transmissibility and contact-pattern assumptions, but the relative ordering of health zones is not.

**Data limitations underlying the estimates:** The analysis explicitly proceeds despite limited data on human mobility, contact patterns, disease transmissibility, tracing and isolation capacity[^okingo-2026-body-10], meaning the population and mobility datasets are used as proxies in the absence of more direct epidemiological or capacity data.

### Limitations

**Limited underlying data:** The authors state that the risk assessment must proceed despite limited data on human mobility, contact patterns, disease transmissibility, tracing and isolation capacity[^okingo-2026-body-10], meaning several key epidemiological inputs are not directly observed and must be approximated.

**Point-estimate instability across assumptions:** The authors note that unlike risk estimates, risk rankings were robust across scenarios and assumptions[^okingo-2026-body-9], implying that the underlying risk-level (point) estimates themselves are not robust and vary depending on the transmissibility and contact-pattern assumptions tested, so only the ranking, not the absolute risk values, should be relied upon.

### Data sources

Primary outbreak data: confirmed case data indicating the outbreak's spatial spread, specifically confirmed cases in Nord-Kivu and Sud-Kivu as of June 4 indicating that the spread has gone beyond the initial focus[^okingo-2026-body-3].

Auxiliary data: population data are from worldpop.org[^okingo-2026-figure-1-introduction-risk-and-sustained-transmission-1], and mobility data are from flowminder.org[^okingo-2026-figure-1-introduction-risk-and-sustained-transmission-2]. No sample sizes for case counts or traveler volumes are reported in the main text.
:::

:::{tab-item} Richard et al. (2 Jun 2026)
### Richard et al. (2 Jun 2026)<br>Assessment of Risk to the U.S. Population from the Ebola Disease Outbreak Caused by Bundibugyo Virus, 2026

[Read the analysis](https://www.cdc.gov/mmwr/volumes/75/wr/mm7522e2.htm)

### Overview

In response to the BVD outbreak in DRC and Uganda, CDC assessed the risk posed by the outbreak to the U.S. population over the next 3 months, using a standardized risk assessment approach that drew on epidemiologic data from the ongoing outbreak and historical data from previous Ebola outbreaks.[^richard-2026-abstract-1] The overall risk was determined by independently assessing two factors: the likelihood of infection and the impact of infection.[^richard-2026-methods-1] The assessment found that the overall risk to the U.S. population is low, based on an extremely low likelihood of transmission, despite the high impact that potential infection could have and the resources that would be required to respond.[^richard-2026-abstract-2]

### Estimate

The overall risk posed by the ongoing BVD outbreak to the U.S. population during the next 3 months is assessed as low, with moderate confidence given the data available.[^richard-2026-results-2] This overall risk reflects a combination of an extremely low likelihood of infection and a high impact of infection for the U.S. population were it to occur.[^richard-2026-results-3]

The relative risk of importation to the United States compared with other locations was estimated at 1.3%[^richard-2026-results-4], derived from the population movement model. No quantitative uncertainty bounds are provided for this figure.

The impact of infection was assessed as high, primarily based on the severity of illness, lack of available medications and vaccines, and resources required to respond; in the two previously identified BVD outbreaks (Uganda 2007 and DRC 2012), case-fatality rates ranged from 25% to 50%.[^richard-2026-results-5]

No outbreak size estimate (total case count or under-detection factor) is reported. The analysis does not produce a central estimate with confidence intervals for total cases.

### Methods

**Standardized qualitative risk assessment framework:** CDC subject matter experts in risk assessment methodology, infectious disease modeling, global health, and Ebola disease collaborated using a standardized risk assessment approach that has been applied to previous viral hemorrhagic fever outbreaks, drawing on available evidence including epidemiologic data from the ongoing BVD outbreak and historical data on Bundibugyo virus and other Ebola disease outbreaks.[^richard-2026-methods-2] The overall risk to the U.S. population was determined by independently assessing two factors: the likelihood of infection (i.e., the probability that members of the U.S. population would acquire Bundibugyo virus infection during the next 3 months, depending on likelihood of exposure, infectiousness, and susceptibility) and the impact of infection (i.e., consequences including disease severity, population immunity, availability of medications and vaccines, and necessary public health response resources).[^richard-2026-methods-3] Experts then assigned a degree of confidence to the assessment, taking into account evidence quality, extent, and corroboration of information.[^richard-2026-methods-4]

**Population movement model for importation risk:** The likelihood of spread from DRC to the United States via infected travelers was assessed using a model that considers population movement; this produced a relative risk of importation to the United States compared with other locations of 1.3%.[^richard-2026-results-1] The method is described only briefly; no calibration procedure, parameter values, or data source for the mobility inputs are specified in the document.

### Assumptions

**Importation likelihood:** The current likelihood of potential spread of BVD from DRC to the United States via travelers who might be infected is considered very low based on modeling results that consider population movement.[^richard-2026-results-8] The specific structural assumptions, parameter values, and data inputs underlying this model are not disclosed in the document.

**Secondary transmission in the United States:** If BVD were introduced into the United States, secondary transmission would likely be minimal, based on historical observation and the known epidemiology of BVD.[^richard-2026-results-9] This assumption relies on the known property that persons are not infectious before symptom onset and that the average interval between cases is long (10–16 days), making public health measures highly effective.[^richard-2026-results-10]

**Population scope:** Risk is assessed only for the general U.S. population, with subpopulations potentially having different likelihood assessments noted separately.[^richard-2026-methods-6] Specifically, the likelihood of infection is judged to be higher among U.S. health care workers practicing in or recently returned from affected regions in DRC and Uganda.[^richard-2026-results-11]

**Case fatality and clinical outcomes:** CFRs of 25%–50% from previous BVD outbreaks are used to characterize disease severity, but the analysis notes that many of those deaths occurred in resource-limited settings and that clinical outcomes might improve with the specialized care available in the United States.[^richard-2026-results-12]

**Outbreak trajectory:** The assessment acknowledges uncertainties about the epidemiology of BVD, the scope and geographic spread of the outbreak, and the potential timelines for implementation of interventions, all of which represent assumptions embedded in the risk tier assigned.[^richard-2026-results-13]

### Limitations

**Limited prior outbreak experience:** Given that only two previous BVD outbreaks have occurred, less is known about Bundibugyo virus than other orthoebolaviruses such as *O. zairense*, which constrains the evidence base for the assessment.[^richard-2026-discussion-1]

**Incomplete outbreak scope:** Limited confirmatory diagnostic testing and challenges in contact tracing have likely resulted in undetected transmission, meaning the full scope of the current outbreak is unclear, making it difficult to assess the potential geographic spread of the outbreak and the subsequent potential for spread to the United States.[^richard-2026-discussion-2]

**Operational and contextual challenges:** Several features of this outbreak pose challenges to assessing its future trajectory, including that the outbreak is occurring in a region with ongoing conflict and unpredictable infrastructure, and the initial large size of the outbreak combined with limited confirmatory diagnostic testing may lead to challenges in infection prevention and control efforts.[^richard-2026-discussion-3]

**General uncertainty:** The assessment recognizes uncertainties around the epidemiology of BVD as well as the current and future scope and geographic spread of the outbreak.[^richard-2026-abstract-4]

### Data sources

**Primary outbreak data:** As of June 2, 2026, a total of 378 confirmed BVD cases and 63 deaths reported in DRC and Uganda, drawn from the two countries' surveillance systems.[^richard-2026-introduction-1]

**Auxiliary data:** Historical data on Bundibugyo virus and other Ebola disease outbreaks were used to inform the assessment.[^richard-2026-methods-5] Specifically, case-fatality rates from the two previous BVD outbreaks in Uganda (2007) and DRC (2012), ranging from 25% to 50%, were referenced.[^richard-2026-results-6] Historical observation of Ebola disease treatment and secondary transmission in the United States, specifically the 2014–2016 West African outbreak during which only 11 persons with Ebola disease were treated in the United States and no community spread occurred, was also used.[^richard-2026-results-7] Modeling results considering population movement informed the relative importation risk estimate of 1.3% for the United States compared with other locations.[^richard-2026-results-1] No specific dataset names, sample sizes for auxiliary sources, or mobility data sources are identified in the document.
:::

:::{tab-item} Fanelli et al. (29 May 2026)
### Fanelli et al. (29 May 2026)<br>Shifting patterns of importation risk of Bundibugyo Ebola virus disease to Europe under outbreak expansion scenarios

[Read the analysis](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.05.31.26354511)

### Overview

Using International Air Transport Association (IATA) origin-destination passenger flows, this analysis estimates the relative risk of Bundibugyo virus disease importation into Europe under six outbreak expansion scenarios reflecting plausible pathways of geographical spread, including cross-border transmission and amplification in highly connected regional capitals.[^fanelli-2026b-abstract-1] Relative exposure patterns across European countries remained largely unchanged under localized transmission in eastern DRC and border-spillover scenarios, but expansion into South Sudan generated a first structural increase in importation pressure to Europe, while hypothetical amplification in Kampala, Kigali, and Kinshasa substantially increased importation pressure and reshaped exposure patterns.[^fanelli-2026b-abstract-2] Across all scenarios, France, Italy, and the United Kingdom remained among the most exposed countries.[^fanelli-2026b-abstract-3]

### Estimate

This analysis does not produce an outbreak size estimate (i.e., a count of cases or infections). Its outputs are relative importation risk rankings for European countries and fold-change increases in importation pressure by European subregion under each scenario. Potential spread across the Ugandan border produced no change in either the relative exposure of European countries or their overall importation pressure compared with the currently reported localized transmission in eastern DRC, with Italy, France, Belgium, and the UK being the most exposed.[^fanelli-2026b-text-3] Expansion to South Sudan produced a first structural increase in importation pressure through Juba (5-fold increase for Northern Europe and 6-fold increase for Eastern Europe relative to localized DRC transmission), while still leaving the UK, France, and Italy among the highest-risk countries.[^fanelli-2026b-text-4] Kampala and Kigali amplification increased exposure in Northern Europe, with Kampala producing a >120-fold increase in importation pressure relative to localized DRC transmission; Kinshasa amplification primarily reinforced exposure in Western Europe (~80-fold increase).[^fanelli-2026b-text-5] In the sensitivity analysis using full-year 2024 travel data, the Kampala amplification scenario produces approximately 190-fold increase for Northern Europe.[^fanelli-2026b-appendix-6]

### Methods

**Air-travel importation risk model using IATA origin-destination flows:** The analysis uses origin-destination passenger flows derived from the 2024 IATA database, which captures complete passenger itineraries including traffic travelling via connecting flights rather than only direct flight segments.[^fanelli-2026b-appendix-2] The relative importation risk to each European country is estimated as the proportion of total outgoing passenger flow from the selected seed airports directed to that country, where passenger flow from each origin airport in a given scenario is summed across all destination airports in the receiving country, then normalized to total passenger flow from source airports to all of Europe.[^fanelli-2026b-appendix-3] Risks are then stratified by European subregion.[^fanelli-2026b-appendix-4] This approach produces relative (not absolute) importation risk rankings and does not incorporate incubation period, detection probability, or infectious prevalence at source. A sensitivity analysis using average monthly data from the full year 2024 was conducted to account for potential seasonal effects; results were broadly consistent with the main analysis, with the exception of the Kampala amplification scenario, in which the increase in importation pressure for Northern Europe rises to approximately 190-fold when using full-year monthly averages.[^fanelli-2026b-appendix-5]

### Assumptions

**Proportional travel as a proxy for importation risk:** The model assumes that the relative proportion of outbound passenger volume from seed airports to each European country is a valid proxy for relative importation risk. The relative importation risk to each destination country is estimated purely as the share of total outgoing passenger flow from the selected seed airports directed to that country, normalized to all Europe-bound flow.[^fanelli-2026b-appendix-3] This structural assumption means that no adjustment is made for incubation period, probability of travelling while infectious, detection at exit or entry, or per-capita incidence at source — the analysis is explicitly relative, not absolute.

**Scenario seeding and airport selection:** The six scenarios are designed to reflect plausible pathways of geographical spread from the currently affected area through porous borders.[^fanelli-2026b-text-6] Each scenario's result depends entirely on which airports are included as seeds: adding or removing an airport changes the importation ranking. The analysis does not specify how the airport catchment areas were defined or how ground-to-air connectivity within source regions was accounted for.

**Travel data representativeness:** The analysis uses cumulative passenger traffic from May, June, and July 2024; this period showed moderate seasonal variability, with mean volumes ranging from 3% below to 16% above the monthly 2024 average across scenarios.[^fanelli-2026b-appendix-8] The analysis implicitly assumes 2024 travel patterns are representative of 2026 patterns during the outbreak period, including any travel disruptions or demand changes associated with the outbreak itself.

**Capital-city amplification as a hypothetical:** Scenarios 4–6 explore hypothetical amplification assuming community transmission in major urban hubs — Kampala, Kigali, and Kinshasa.[^fanelli-2026b-text-7] The analysis does not assign probabilities to these scenarios occurring; they are treated as illustrative of what importation pressure would look like if amplification did occur, rather than as probabilistic forecasts.

### Limitations

**Relative risk only, no absolute importation probability:** The analysis produces relative rankings and fold-change comparisons but does not estimate the absolute probability that any infected traveller departs or arrives at a given destination. Patterns of importation risk to Europe may rapidly shift under uncertainty surrounding the geographical extent and evolution of the outbreak, particularly if transmission expands into major urban hubs with strong international connectivity near the affected area.[^fanelli-2026b-text-8] The analysis does not quantify this uncertainty in probabilistic terms.

**Seasonal and temporal representativeness of travel data:** The analyses use cumulative passenger traffic from May, June, and July 2024, a period that showed moderate seasonal variability relative to the 2024 annual average.[^fanelli-2026b-appendix-8] The authors do not discuss whether outbreak-related changes in travel behaviour (e.g., reduced tourism or business travel to affected regions) would alter the estimates.

**No adjustment for detection, infectious period, or per-capita incidence:** The method does not incorporate incubation period distributions, probability of travelling while infectious, exit or entry screening effectiveness, or source-area incidence rates. As a consequence, all scenarios implicitly assume equal per-traveller exportation risk from each seed airport, which the authors do not explicitly flag as a limitation but is a direct consequence of the proportional-flow methodology.

**Hypothetical nature of amplification scenarios:** Scenarios 4–6 explore hypothetical amplification assuming community transmission in major urban hubs.[^fanelli-2026b-text-7] The analysis does not assign likelihoods to these scenarios or model the transmission dynamics required to reach that state, limiting their direct operational applicability.

### Data sources

**Primary outbreak data:** As of 29 May 2026, the outbreak involved 125 confirmed cases (17 deaths) and 906 suspected cases (223 deaths) across Ituri, North Kivu, and South Kivu provinces in Eastern DRC, and 9 confirmed cases (1 death) in Uganda.[^fanelli-2026b-text-1] These figures inform the scenario definitions (which areas to include as seeds) but are not directly used as a quantitative input to the model formula.

**Auxiliary data:** Origin-destination passenger flows are derived from the 2024 IATA database, which captures complete passenger itineraries including connecting flights.[^fanelli-2026b-appendix-2] Analyses are based on cumulative passenger traffic reported for May, June, and July 2024.[^fanelli-2026b-appendix-1] Relative to annual traffic to Europe, mean passenger volumes during May–July ranged from 3% below to 16% above the monthly 2024 average across scenarios.[^fanelli-2026b-appendix-7] No line list, demographic data, or parameters borrowed from prior outbreaks are used as model inputs; the method relies solely on the IATA travel volumes and the airport-level scenario seeding definitions.
:::

:::{tab-item} van Zandvoort et al. (20 May 2026)
### van Zandvoort et al. (20 May 2026)<br>The risk of global Ebola virus spread is low: epidemiology of Ebola disease cases outside Africa, 1976 to May 2026

[Read the analysis](https://www.eurosurveillance.org/content/10.2807/1560-7917.ES.2026.31.24.2600508)

### Overview

This analysis reviews the historical record of confirmed Ebola disease cases presenting outside Africa to contextualize the intercontinental spread risk posed by the 2026 Bundibugyo virus disease (BVD) outbreak in DRC. The authors conducted internet searches — both manual and AI-assisted — to identify all laboratory-confirmed Ebola disease cases presenting outside Africa, without any time limit.[^van-zandvoort-2026-ebola-disease-cases-outside-africa-1] The main finding is that intercontinental transmission risk remains low, based on the full historical record of such cases.[^van-zandvoort-2026-summary-1]

### Estimate

The analysis does not produce an outbreak size estimate for the 2026 BVD outbreak itself. Instead it reports importation risk metrics derived from the historical record.

The crude overall risk since 2000 is 0.81 cases outside Africa per 1,000 Ebola disease cases in a source outbreak (including medically evacuated, latent, and secondary cases); focusing on latent cases and their secondary cases only, the rate is 0.17 cases outside Africa per 1,000 source outbreak cases.[^van-zandvoort-2026-low-overall-risk-of-exportation-2] This represents the empirically observed risk across all countries outside Africa combined; per-country risks are smaller — for example, for the UK alone, the estimate is 0.03 latent cases per 1,000 source outbreak cases.[^van-zandvoort-2026-low-overall-risk-of-exportation-3]

From the time-varying analysis of the 2014–16 epidemic, exportation risk per reported source case decreased by 4.6% per week (95% CI: 1.7–7.4%; p = 0.0097), and was substantially lower after the epidemic peak (0.46; 95% CI: 0.17–0.73 exported cases per 1,000 source cases) than before it (1.7; 95% CI: 0.95–2.7 exported cases per 1,000 source cases).[^van-zandvoort-2026-decrease-in-exportation-risk-over-the-3]

### Methods

**Historical case review and crude risk calculation:** The authors identified and analysed all known Ebola disease cases outside Africa to contextualise intercontinental spread, using manual and AI-assisted internet searches of scientific articles, public health bulletins, and news reports.[^van-zandvoort-2026-summary-3] As a crude risk measure, they calculated the number of cases occurring outside Africa per 1,000 reported cases in the originating epidemic for outbreaks since 2000, producing separate estimates including and excluding medically evacuated cases.[^van-zandvoort-2026-low-overall-risk-of-exportation-1] This calculation applies globally across all destination countries outside Africa combined, with a UK-specific example provided at the national level.

**Time-varying Poisson regression:** For cases exported from the 2014–16 West African epidemic specifically, the authors conducted a time-varying risk analysis using Poisson regression of the weekly count of exported cases by country (Guinea, Liberia, Sierra Leone) against the number of cases reported that week and a linear time trend.[^van-zandvoort-2026-decrease-in-exportation-risk-over-the-1] This produced an estimate of how exported case counts scaled with source-country incidence, with no significant deviation from linear scaling found.[^van-zandvoort-2026-decrease-in-exportation-risk-over-the-2] This method applies to the 2014–16 epidemic as a historical comparator and is not applied directly to the 2026 BVD outbreak.

### Assumptions

**Linear scaling of exportation risk with source incidence:** The Poisson regression assumes that exported case counts scale linearly with weekly source-country incidence, an assumption the analysis found consistent with the 2014–16 data (estimated scaling: 0.98; 95% CI: 0.60–1.36; p = 0.86).[^van-zandvoort-2026-decrease-in-exportation-risk-over-the-2] This assumption underpins the use of crude per-1,000-cases risk ratios to project forward.

**Crude risk ratio as applicable across outbreaks:** The crude risk measure is calculated for Ebola disease outbreaks since the year 2000[^van-zandvoort-2026-low-overall-risk-of-exportation-4], implicitly assuming that the historical ratio of exported-to-source cases is informative for the current 2026 BVD outbreak. The analysis does not explicitly state whether this assumption holds given differences in travel patterns, response capacity, or the specific virus species involved.

**Distinction between medically evacuated and latent cases:** The analysis distinguishes between medically evacuated cases, who represent known risks manageable with strict biosecurity, and latent cases arriving via commercial flights who need to be diagnosed before isolation.[^van-zandvoort-2026-discussion-2] The risk ratios are computed separately for these two categories, with the latent-only figure treated as the more policy-relevant measure of uncontrolled importation risk.

**Completeness of historical case ascertainment:** Agreement among search methods and with a prior clinical review supports that the case list is likely complete, but potential under-ascertainment may arise from underrepresentation of public health bulletins not published in English, and from possible geographic biases in web-based search tools.[^van-zandvoort-2026-discussion-3]

### Limitations

**Potential under-ascertainment of exported cases:** Public health bulletins not published in English were underrepresented despite targeted native-language searches, and web-based search tools may have returned geographically biased results, potentially leading to missed cases.[^van-zandvoort-2026-discussion-4]

**Historical data may not reflect current outbreak conditions:** The analysis applies risk ratios derived primarily from the 2014–16 West African epidemic — a very large, prolonged epidemic with substantial international humanitarian response — to the 2026 BVD context. The authors note that the decrease in exportation risk over the 2014–16 epidemic is likely most consistent with improvement in infection prevention and control measures coinciding with the stepping-up of response efforts in late 2014[^van-zandvoort-2026-decrease-in-exportation-risk-over-the-5], but do not formally assess whether this dynamic applies to the current, structurally different outbreak.

**Per-country risks not estimated:** The crude risk estimate represents the empirically observed risk across all countries outside Africa; per-country risks are smaller[^van-zandvoort-2026-low-overall-risk-of-exportation-5], and the analysis provides only the aggregate outside-Africa figure plus a single UK example, without ranking or estimating risk for individual destination countries.

**Latent case detection depends on screening:** The four latent cases identified occurred among 300,000 screened travellers and were all asymptomatic at exit and entry screening; their eventual identification relied on screening protocols that included guidance on self-monitoring of symptoms.[^van-zandvoort-2026-discussion-5] The analysis does not assess what the latent case rate would have been in the absence of such protocols.

### Data sources

**Primary outbreak data:** The analysis identified 28 confirmed Ebola disease cases outside Africa in total, of which 27 occurred during the 2014–16 West African epidemic and one has occurred so far during the 2026 BVD epidemic.[^van-zandvoort-2026-ebola-disease-cases-outside-africa-6] Sources consulted included 27 scientific articles, 9 public health bulletins, 82 news articles, and 9 other sites.[^van-zandvoort-2026-ebola-disease-cases-outside-africa-7]

**Auxiliary data:** Weekly case counts for Guinea, Liberia, and Sierra Leone (total 28,592 confirmed, probable, and suspected cases) covering the 2014–16 epidemic were used as the source-country incidence series in the Poisson regression.[^van-zandvoort-2026-decrease-in-exportation-risk-over-the-4] The authors also reference the approximately 300,000 travellers screened by CDC-supported exit screening programmes in Guinea, Liberia, and Sierra Leone during the 2014–16 epidemic, as context for the four identified latent cases.[^van-zandvoort-2026-discussion-1] Findings were checked against existing lists of Ebola disease cases.[^van-zandvoort-2026-ebola-disease-cases-outside-africa-8]
:::

:::{dropdown} Methods overview

These pages are produced by a semi-automated evidence-synthesis pipeline that pairs AI models with human review at each major checkpoint: one model reads each source analysis on its own and grounds every extracted claim in a verbatim quoted passage; a second model takes the resulting structured comparison as input, and drafts the narrative. Human analysts then review and edit the output to ensure accuracy and completeness prior to publishing.

**Identifying analyses.** Analyses are identified through continuous monitoring of preprint servers, peer-reviewed publications, and grey literature. An analysis is included if it directly addresses the 2026 Bundibugyo virus disease (BVD) outbreak and provides a quantitative estimate relevant to a given topic (e.g., outbreak size, risk of spread). Each included source is logged, and if the authors later release an updated version, the newer version supersedes the earlier one.

**Preparing the source text.** Each source text, regardless of source type (e.g., PDF, HTML), is converted to Markdown. A human analyst then curates the converted file and removes sections that carry no evidentiary content, such as references, acknowledgments, author contributions, affiliations, and declarations of interest, to keep extraction focused and reduce the number of input tokens used.

**Extracting structured evidence.** The curated text is split into individually citeable units, one per sentence, and passed to Claude Sonnet 5-5. For each of a fixed set of dimensions (e.g., estimates, methods, limitations), the model answers from that one source alone and must ground every factual claim in a verbatim span of its text.

**Synthesizing the narrative.** OpenAI's GPT-5.5 model reads the dimensions extracted for each analysis and synthesizes the information, covering the state of the evidence, how the estimates compare and why they diverge, the key uncertainties and assumptions shared across analyses, what would most reduce that uncertainty, and what it implies for the response.

**Human review and publication.** Human analysts edit the draft for accuracy, completeness, and tone prior to publishing.

**Limitations.** This pipeline surfaces only publicly available analyses; works that have not been made public are not captured.

:::

Tambo International Airport (Supplementary Table 1)."

[^kinoshita-2026-airline-flow-based-network-and-effective-distance-for-13]: [Kinoshita, Suzuki, & Yoneoka (2026) "Airline-flow-based network and effective distance for hazard estimation"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.06.01.26354569): "The sensitivity analysis shows that the probability had a similar geographical tendency, even if the starting airport is assumed to be EBB: the mean (standard deviation) ranged from 0.170 (0.049) to 0.616 (0.099) (Supplementary Figures 1-5 and Supplementary Table 2)."
[^kinoshita-2026-abstract-4]: [Kinoshita, Suzuki, & Yoneoka (2026) "Abstract"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.06.01.26354569): "We projected the potential airline-mediated importation risk during the 2026 DRC-Uganda outbreak using a hazard-based model informed by contemporary airline transportation networks and a hazard parameter calibrated from the 2013-2016 West African Ebola virus disease (EVD) epidemic, one of the few EBOD outbreaks with documented long-distance airline-mediated international exportations."
[^kinoshita-2026-airline-flow-based-network-and-effective-distance-for-4]: [Kinoshita, Suzuki, & Yoneoka (2026) "Airline-flow-based network and effective distance for hazard estimation"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.06.01.26354569): "To model the global spread on airline network, we first constructed a directed, weighted air-traffic network using airport-level origin-destination passenger-flow data from OAG for May 2024. OAG is a global travel data provider listed in the IATA Strategic Partners Directory and provides aviation schedules, status data, and analytics products for the air-travel ecosystem. The network includes 11,264 airports (nodes), 569,741 routes (edges) and 329,326,078 passengers."
[^kinoshita-2026-airline-flow-based-network-and-effective-distance-for-5]: [Kinoshita, Suzuki, & Yoneoka (2026) "Airline-flow-based network and effective distance for hazard estimation"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.06.01.26354569): "Given a single airport-level adjacency matrix *A* , where *A* *ij* denotes the number of passenger volume travelling from airport *i* to *j* during May 2024, the shortest-path effective distance from Kinshasa/N'djili International Airport (IATA code: FIH) to airport *j* was defined by , where *Γ* *ij* indicates the set of all directed paths from airport *i* to *j* and *γ* is one path. Shortest paths were calculated on the directed network using Dijkstra's algorithm."
[^kinoshita-2026-airline-flow-based-network-and-effective-distance-for-6]: [Kinoshita, Suzuki, & Yoneoka (2026) "Airline-flow-based network and effective distance for hazard estimation"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.06.01.26354569): "Then, to estimate the arrival probability using the effective distance, we modeled the arrival hazard at airport *j* as *λ* *j* = θ/ *D* *j* , where *θ* = 0.19 is used as the primary value based on a previous EBOD importation hazard model. The arrival probability that infection reaches airport *j* within *t* days is calculated by *p* *j* ( *t* ) = 1 - exp(- *tλ* *j* )."
[^kinoshita-2026-airline-flow-based-network-and-effective-distance-for-7]: [Kinoshita, Suzuki, & Yoneoka (2026) "Airline-flow-based network and effective distance for hazard estimation"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.06.01.26354569): "Further, the country-level arrival risk was summarized as the maximum airport-level probability within each country: , where *A* *c* denotes the set of airports located in country *c* ."
[^kinoshita-2026-airline-flow-based-network-and-effective-distance-for-8]: [Kinoshita, Suzuki, & Yoneoka (2026) "Airline-flow-based network and effective distance for hazard estimation"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.06.01.26354569): "As the sensitivity analysis, we checked *θ* = {0.06, 0.32} based on the 95% confidence interval of."
[^kinoshita-2026-airline-flow-based-network-and-effective-distance-for-9]: [Kinoshita, Suzuki, & Yoneoka (2026) "Airline-flow-based network and effective distance for hazard estimation"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.06.01.26354569): "In the sensitivity analysis, *δ* can be also estimated from, where *r* is the epidemic growth rate ( *r* = {0.034, 0.05, 0.1} estimated as of May 17, 2026, *γ* *e* is the Euler-Mascheroni constant ( *γ* e ≈ 0.58), and *α* FIH is the outbound passenger flow from FIH divided by the assumed FIH catchment population *α* FIH ≈ 0.0001."
[^kinoshita-2026-airline-flow-based-network-and-effective-distance-for-16]: [Kinoshita, Suzuki, & Yoneoka (2026) "Airline-flow-based network and effective distance for hazard estimation"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.06.01.26354569): "Here, we assume that the relationship between effective distance and EVD importation observed in 2013-2016 was transferable to the current BVD outbreak. The estimated effective distance distribution was very similar to that in the previous study, supporting the use of same *θ* value on a comparable hazard scale."
[^kinoshita-2026-airline-flow-based-network-and-effective-distance-for-17]: [Kinoshita, Suzuki, & Yoneoka (2026) "Airline-flow-based network and effective distance for hazard estimation"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.06.01.26354569): "In the main analyses, disease-specific values of *δ* was set to 1, which assumes that the each additional travel step contributes one unit of distance, and thus corresponds to the Brockmann-Helbing scale. This scale has been empirically shown to effectively predict the global spread patterns of infectious diseases."
[^kinoshita-2026-airline-flow-based-network-and-effective-distance-for-18]: [Kinoshita, Suzuki, & Yoneoka (2026) "Airline-flow-based network and effective distance for hazard estimation"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.06.01.26354569): "In the sensitivity analysis, *δ* can be also estimated from, where *r* is the epidemic growth rate ( *r* = {0.034, 0.05, 0.1} estimated as of May 17, 2026, *γ* *e* is the Euler-Mascheroni constant ( *γ* e ≈ 0.58), and *α* FIH is the outbound passenger flow from FIH divided by the assumed FIH catchment population *α* FIH ≈ 0.0001. While this *δ* formulation allows us to incorporate information about the current epidemic situation by adding more parameters, it also increases uncertainty."
[^kinoshita-2026-airline-flow-based-network-and-effective-distance-for-19]: [Kinoshita, Suzuki, & Yoneoka (2026) "Airline-flow-based network and effective distance for hazard estimation"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.06.01.26354569): "Sensitivity analyses using the lower and upper bounds of the historical parameter changed absolute arrival probabilities but broadly preserved the overall pattern of higher-probability airports. This is expected because effective distance determines the spatial pattern, whereas the hazard parameter mainly scales the probabilies."
[^kinoshita-2026-airline-flow-based-network-and-effective-distance-for-20]: [Kinoshita, Suzuki, & Yoneoka (2026) "Airline-flow-based network and effective distance for hazard estimation"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.06.01.26354569): "Our estimates also relied on 2024 airline network data and therefore may not capture subsequent changes in routes, passenger volumes, airspace restrictions, or travel advisories, including those affecting routes through Russia and the Middle East."

[^kinoshita-2026-airline-flow-based-network-and-effective-distance-for-24]: [Kinoshita, Suzuki, & Yoneoka (2026) "Airline-flow-based network and effective distance for hazard estimation"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.06.01.26354569): "Our estimates also relied on 2024 airline network data and therefore may not capture subsequent changes in routes, passenger volumes, airspace restrictions, or travel advisories, including those affecting routes through Russia and the Middle East. The projections also did not explicitly account for post-PHEIC interventions, enhanced screening or behavioural changes."
[^kinoshita-2026-airline-flow-based-network-and-effective-distance-for-25]: [Kinoshita, Suzuki, & Yoneoka (2026) "Airline-flow-based network and effective distance for hazard estimation"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.06.01.26354569): "Estimated arrival probability should be interpreted as importation risk rather than the risk of local transmission after arrival."
[^kinoshita-2026-airline-flow-based-network-and-effective-distance-for-26]: [Kinoshita, Suzuki, & Yoneoka (2026) "Airline-flow-based network and effective distance for hazard estimation"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.06.01.26354569): "Thus, countries with high-probability airport gateways should be interpreted as priorities for awareness and preparedness, rather than as locations where local outbreaks are expected."
[^kinoshita-2026-abstract-3]: [Kinoshita, Suzuki, & Yoneoka (2026) "Abstract"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.06.01.26354569): "As of 4 June 2026, the DRC had reported 363 confirmed cases, including 62 deaths. Uganda had reported 15 confirmed cases, including one death."
[^kinoshita-2026-airline-flow-based-network-and-effective-distance-for-14]: [Kinoshita, Suzuki, & Yoneoka (2026) "Airline-flow-based network and effective distance for hazard estimation"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.06.01.26354569): "Because no airline-mediated importation had been reported so far as of 28 May 2026, we used a hazard parameter estimated using the 2013-2016 West African EVD epidemic."
[^kinoshita-2026-airline-flow-based-network-and-effective-distance-for-15]: [Kinoshita, Suzuki, & Yoneoka (2026) "Airline-flow-based network and effective distance for hazard estimation"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.06.01.26354569): "Then, to estimate the arrival probability using the effective distance, we modeled the arrival hazard at airport *j* as *λ* *j* = θ/ *D* *j* , where *θ* = 0.19 is used as the primary value based on a previous EBOD importation hazard model."
[^walekhwa-2026-abstract-1]: [Walekhwa et al. (2026) "Abstract"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.06.22.26356210): "** We constructed a data-driven directed weighted mobility network using IOM Displacement Tracking Matrix (DTM) flows collected from 15–24 May 2026 (11,245 observed movements) and the 2024 Uganda census (45.9 million people). A stochastic metapopulation SEIR model, incorporating pre-symptomatic transmission and movement of exposed and infectious individuals, was simulated over 90 days across 135 Ugandan districts and two DRC provinces."
[^walekhwa-2026-abstract-2]: [Walekhwa et al. (2026) "Abstract"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.06.22.26356210): "Under the containment scenario (effective R = 0.35), the model projected a median of 22 cumulative cases (95% CrI: 22–24) and 2 deaths (95% CrI: 2–4) in Kampala over 90 days. All other 134 districts had a median of 0 cases. Non-pharmaceutical interventions at high, moderate, and low compliance produced no statistically significant reduction in cases (22 cases across all scenarios)."
[^walekhwa-2026-3-results-1]: [Walekhwa et al. (2026) "3. Results"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.06.22.26356210): "Under the containment scenario (effective R = 0.35), the model projected a median of 22 cumulative cases (95% CrI: 22–24) and 2 deaths (95% CrI: 2–4) over 90 days, all concentrated in Kampala (**Table 3**). The median total cases ranged from 22 to 22 across compliance scenarios, with 2 deaths, 3 HCW infections, and 0 HCW deaths. The 95% credible intervals overlapped substantially, indicating no statistically significant difference between compliance levels."
[^walekhwa-2026-3-results-2]: [Walekhwa et al. (2026) "3. Results"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.06.22.26356210): "Under the containment scenario, Kampala was the only district with a non-zero median projection, bearing a predicted burden of 22 cases (95% CrI: 22–24) and a 100% probability of a local outbreak (>10 cases). All other 134 districts had a median of 0 predicted cases (upper credible intervals extending to 1 in a small fraction of simulations)."
[^walekhwa-2026-2-methods-12]: [Walekhwa et al. (2026) "2. Methods"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.06.22.26356210): "The containment scenario (used for district-level predictions) had effective *R*₀ = 0.35, mobility scale 0.05 (95% reduction), and suppression strength 100. Moderate compliance used *R*₀ = 0.40, mobility scale 0.08, and suppression strength 50. Low compliance used *R*₀ = 0.45, mobility scale 0.12, and suppression strength 30."
[^walekhwa-2026-2-methods-4]: [Walekhwa et al. (2026) "2. Methods"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.06.22.26356210): "We built both undirected and directed weighted networks from the DTM flow data using the igraph package in R. For the undirected network, we summed reciprocal flows for each unordered pair of locations to obtain a single undirected edge weight. For the directed network, we preserved the original flow directions without collapsing reciprocal edges, allowing computation of in-strength (import risk) and out-strength (export risk) separately. We then added all 135 Ugandan districts and the two DRC provinces as nodes. Districts that did not appear in any flow record remained as isolated nodes with no edges."
[^walekhwa-2026-2-methods-5]: [Walekhwa et al. (2026) "2. Methods"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.06.22.26356210): "To impute 12-month movement estimates from the 10-day observed flows, we multiplied the observed weights by 36.5. To assess uncertainty due to the limited 10-day sampling period, we performed bootstrap resampling with 1,000 replicates."
[^walekhwa-2026-2-methods-6]: [Walekhwa et al. (2026) "2. Methods"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.06.22.26356210): "We developed a discrete-time, stochastic SEIR model at the node level. Each node maintained five compartments: Susceptible (*S*), Exposed (*E*), Infectious (*I*), Recovered (*R*), and Dead (*D*). The latent period was set to 7.5 days and the infectious period to 8.5 days, giving daily progression probabilities *σ* = 1/7.5 and *γ* = 1/8.5."
[^walekhwa-2026-2-methods-7]: [Walekhwa et al. (2026) "2. Methods"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.06.22.26356210): "Pre-symptomatic transmission was modelled by setting the relative infectiousness of exposed individuals to 0.5 compared to infectious individuals, based on estimates from early EVD outbreaks and the empirical observation that six of the secondary cases in Kampala tested positive while under quarantine."
[^walekhwa-2026-2-methods-8]: [Walekhwa et al. (2026) "2. Methods"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.06.22.26356210): "The baseline transmission rate *β* was derived from the basic reproduction number *R*₀ = 2.76, calibrated to the early growth of the outbreak (16 cases in 21 days), using *β* = *R*₀ / infectious period."
[^walekhwa-2026-2-methods-9]: [Walekhwa et al. (2026) "2. Methods"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.06.22.26356210): "The DRC provinces were modelled as explicit nodes with their own SEIR dynamics. We recalibrated the DRC model using actual reported data as of May 25, 2026: 381 confirmed cases, 2,987 exposed contacts, and 64 deaths. Nord-Kivu was initialised with 4,000 exposed and 400 infectious individuals, and Ituri with 500 exposed and 50 infectious, to reproduce these numbers."
[^walekhwa-2026-abstract-5]: [Walekhwa et al. (2026) "Abstract"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.06.22.26356210): "The 7-1-7 agile response was explicitly modelled with dynamic contact tracing coverage (40% → 70% → 85%)."
[^walekhwa-2026-2-methods-10]: [Walekhwa et al. (2026) "2. Methods"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.06.22.26356210): "To quantify the influence of parameter uncertainty on the total cumulative case count, we performed a global sensitivity analysis using the Sobol method. The first-order sensitivity index *S*ᵢ measures the fraction of output variance caused by parameter *i* alone, while the total-order index *T*ᵢ measures the fraction caused by parameter *i* and all its interactions with other parameters:."
[^walekhwa-2026-2-methods-11]: [Walekhwa et al. (2026) "2. Methods"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.06.22.26356210): "The design used quasi-random Sobol sequences with *N* = 500 samples, leading to 500 × (11 + 2) = 6,500 model evaluations. For each parameter set, we averaged the output over 3 stochastic replicates to reduce noise. The Sobol indices were then estimated with 200 bootstrap replicates to obtain 95% confidence intervals."
[^walekhwa-2026-2-methods-17]: [Walekhwa et al. (2026) "2. Methods"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.06.22.26356210): "We made the following key assumptions: The DTM flow data (10-day period) are representative of average daily movement patterns after scaling by 36.5."
[^walekhwa-2026-2-methods-18]: [Walekhwa et al. (2026) "2. Methods"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.06.22.26356210): "Movement is independent each day (no memory); only exposed and infectious individuals move, and the probability of moving is the same for both compartments."
[^walekhwa-2026-4-discussion-1]: [Walekhwa et al. (2026) "4. Discussion"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.06.22.26356210): "The DTM data cover only a 10-day window and eight border crossing points; movements through unofficial crossings or after the outbreak escalated may not be captured, a limitation common to mobility-based epidemiological studies."
[^walekhwa-2026-2-methods-19]: [Walekhwa et al. (2026) "2. Methods"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.06.22.26356210): "The latent period was set to 7.5 days and the infectious period to 8.5 days, giving daily progression probabilities *σ* = 1/7.5 and *γ* = 1/8.5."
[^walekhwa-2026-2-methods-20]: [Walekhwa et al. (2026) "2. Methods"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.06.22.26356210): "The case fatality ratio (2/20 = 0.10) and HCW attack rate (3/20 = 0.15) remain constant."
[^walekhwa-2026-2-methods-21]: [Walekhwa et al. (2026) "2. Methods"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.06.22.26356210): "The DRC case fatality ratio was set to 64/381 = 0.168, based on reported outbreak data."
[^walekhwa-2026-2-methods-22]: [Walekhwa et al. (2026) "2. Methods"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.06.22.26356210): "The detection delay of 3 days applies uniformly to all infected individuals, and quarantine after detection is perfect."
[^walekhwa-2026-2-methods-23]: [Walekhwa et al. (2026) "2. Methods"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.06.22.26356210): "Reported incidence was the number of new cases on the previous day multiplied by the detection probability *p*detect = 1.0 (assuming all symptomatic cases are detected)."
[^walekhwa-2026-2-methods-24]: [Walekhwa et al. (2026) "2. Methods"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.06.22.26356210): "The DRC epidemic curve is independent of Uganda's interventions and drives importations solely through the movement matrix."
[^walekhwa-2026-2-methods-25]: [Walekhwa et al. (2026) "2. Methods"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.06.22.26356210): "DRC response parameters were set to represent a moderately delayed response: detection delay 7 days (based on the 2018–2020 DRC outbreak), notification delay 3 days, response delay 5 days, contact tracing coverage 30%, detection probability 0.7, and no behavioural response (*k* = 1)."
[^walekhwa-2026-2-methods-26]: [Walekhwa et al. (2026) "2. Methods"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.06.22.26356210): "Compliance with NPIs is constant over the 90 days and homogeneous across districts."
[^walekhwa-2026-2-methods-3]: [Walekhwa et al. (2026) "2. Methods"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.06.22.26356210): "No importations from countries other than DRC are considered."
[^walekhwa-2026-2-methods-27]: [Walekhwa et al. (2026) "2. Methods"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.06.22.26356210): "At each daily time step, the algorithm: computed the time-dependent mobility decay factor based on cumulative Ugandan cases; moved exposed and infectious individuals according to **M**ₜ using binomial draws; calculated the force of infection for each node, incorporating the behavioural response and the NPI multipliers; drew new exposures (binomial), new infections (negative binomial for superspreading, with dispersion parameter *k*disp = 0.3), natural recoveries, and deaths (CFR = 2/20 = 0.10 for Uganda, 64/381 = 0.168 for DRC); applied dynamic contact tracing (coverage based on cumulative cases); and updated all compartments."
[^walekhwa-2026-4-discussion-2]: [Walekhwa et al. (2026) "4. Discussion"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.06.22.26356210): "The homogeneous mixing assumption within districts likely overestimates transmission in low-density areas and underestimates it in high-density slums."
[^walekhwa-2026-4-discussion-4]: [Walekhwa et al. (2026) "4. Discussion"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.06.22.26356210): "The Sobol confidence intervals for some parameters remained wide because the small outbreak size (median 22 cases) produces low output variance, making precise quantitative ranking unstable, hence our qualitative interpretation."
[^walekhwa-2026-4-discussion-5]: [Walekhwa et al. (2026) "4. Discussion"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.06.22.26356210): "Hospital and funeral transmission are not explicitly modelled, a limitation given that Legrand et al. (2007) identified nosocomial transmission and unsafe burials as major amplifiers in Ebola outbreaks."
[^walekhwa-2026-4-discussion-6]: [Walekhwa et al. (2026) "4. Discussion"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.06.22.26356210): "Importations from neighbouring countries other than DRC were not considered, though the 79.9% importation risk estimated by Chalma et al. (2026) focused specifically on DRC-origin introductions."
[^walekhwa-2026-2-methods-13]: [Walekhwa et al. (2026) "2. Methods"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.06.22.26356210): "First, the Ministry of Health EVD situation report as of June 5, 2026, provided the initial outbreak conditions. The report confirmed 15 cumulative cases (plus 1 probable), 2 deaths, 5 recoveries, and 720 cumulative contacts listed (455 active under follow-up). All initial cases were located in Kampala and were under institutional quarantine."
[^walekhwa-2026-2-methods-14]: [Walekhwa et al. (2026) "2. Methods"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.06.22.26356210): "We recalibrated the DRC model using actual reported data as of May 25, 2026: 381 confirmed cases, 2,987 exposed contacts, and 64 deaths. Nord-Kivu was initialised with 4,000 exposed and 400 infectious individuals, and Ituri with 500 exposed and 50 infectious, to reproduce these numbers."
[^walekhwa-2026-2-methods-15]: [Walekhwa et al. (2026) "2. Methods"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.06.22.26356210): "Second, the IOM DTM Flow Monitoring report covering 15–24 May 2026 summarised cross-border movements from DRC into Uganda and internal movements among Ugandan districts. From the report we extracted the total observed individuals (11,245) and the percentage distribution across eight Flow Monitoring Points (FMPs): Ntoroko Main (28%), Mpondwe (18%), Busunga (18%), Bunagana (12%), Cyanika (8%), Goli (8%), Vurra (3%), and Busanza (3%)."
[^walekhwa-2026-2-methods-16]: [Walekhwa et al. (2026) "2. Methods"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.06.22.26356210): "Third, the official 2024 Uganda Population and Housing Census final report (UBOS, December 2024) gave the de facto population for each of the 135 districts. The total national population was 45,905,417."
[^walekhwa-2026-research-in-context-1]: [Walekhwa et al. (2026) "Research in Context"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.06.22.26356210): "Previous outbreaks in Uganda (2007–08; 131 confirmed cases, 42 deaths) and DR Congo (2012; 38 confirmed cases, 13 deaths) suggested lower transmissibility and case-fatality ratio than Zaire ebolavirus, with reproduction number estimates ranging from 1.2 to 2.6."
[^walekhwa-2026-research-in-context-2]: [Walekhwa et al. (2026) "Research in Context"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.06.22.26356210): "Concurrently, Bangelesa and colleagues conducted a population mobility mapping exercise in Ituri Province using monthly data from Flowminder (March 2025–March 2026) and computed a normalised mobility intensity index (MII) for all 340 health zones. Their analysis revealed that Ituri Province had the second highest average MII nationally (25.3), behind only Kinshasa (26.6), and 2.4 times the national median (10.7). Critically, the three outbreak epicentre health zones, Rwampara (MII 68.6, rank 2), Bunia (MII 62.6, rank 3), and Mongbwalu (MII 49.9, rank 11), all exceeded the 95th national percentile (41.4), underscoring the exceptional connectivity of the affected zones."
[^bouhentala-2026-abstract-1]: [Bouhentala & Kadir (2026) "Abstract"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.07.19.26358411): "Risk was assessed across introduction likelihood, conditional onward-transmission likelihood, impact, and confidence."
[^bouhentala-2026-3-results-4]: [Bouhentala & Kadir (2026) "3. Results"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.07.19.26358411): "As of 15 July 2026, the DRC had reported 2,124 laboratory-confirmed cases, 828 deaths among confirmed cases, 390 reported recoveries, and 725 patients hospitalised in isolation. Confirmed cases had been reported from 46 of 140 health zones across five provinces."
[^bouhentala-2026-3-results-5]: [Bouhentala & Kadir (2026) "3. Results"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.07.19.26358411): "Excluding the 29 June – 1 July interval, the aggregate rate was 35.9 per day during 2–29 June (970 notifications over 27 days; exact counting interval 33.7–38.3) and 47.4 per day during 1–15 July (664 over 14 days; 43.9–51.2). The July-to-June notification-rate ratio was 1.32 (counting-model 95% interval 1.20–1.46)."
[^bouhentala-2026-3-results-6]: [Bouhentala & Kadir (2026) "3. Results"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.07.19.26358411): "ECDC estimated approximately one importation per 23,000–24,000 travellers from the principal outbreak region, with a 90% uncertainty interval of one per 13,000–54,000. Under a hypothetical scenario of 100 travellers during 11–25 June, ECDC estimated a 0.45% probability of at least one importation (90% uncertainty interval 0.20%–0.85%)."
[^bouhentala-2026-2-methods-4]: [Bouhentala & Kadir (2026) "2. Methods"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.07.19.26358411): "This was a rapid public-health analysis of publicly available aggregate surveillance data and official risk assessments. The principal sources were WHO Disease Outbreak News, WHO/IHR statements and technical guidance, ECDC surveillance and modelling reports, WHO regional offices, national ministry-of-health dashboards and updates, and peer-reviewed primary studies for historical BDBV outbreaks, viral persistence, and candidate countermeasures."
[^bouhentala-2026-2-methods-5]: [Bouhentala & Kadir (2026) "2. Methods"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.07.19.26358411): "Airline sources were used only to establish the existence of plausible routes and not to estimate passenger volumes."
[^bouhentala-2026-2-methods-6]: [Bouhentala & Kadir (2026) "2. Methods"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.07.19.26358411): "Cumulative confirmed DRC counts were indexed by reporting date rather than symptom-onset date. Interval-specific average notification rates were calculated as the change in cumulative confirmed cases divided by the number of days between reports. Exact 95% Poisson counting intervals were calculated using the Garwood method."
[^bouhentala-2026-2-methods-7]: [Bouhentala & Kadir (2026) "2. Methods"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.07.19.26358411): "The notification-rate ratio comparing 1–15 July with 2–29 June was calculated as (C₁/t₁)/(C₀/t₀). The 95% interval was estimated on the log scale using SE[log(RR)] = √(1/C₁ + 1/C₀)."
[^bouhentala-2026-2-methods-8]: [Bouhentala & Kadir (2026) "2. Methods"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.07.19.26358411): "No reproduction number was estimated because onset-date line-list data were unavailable."
[^bouhentala-2026-2-methods-9]: [Bouhentala & Kadir (2026) "2. Methods"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.07.19.26358411): "Risk was separated into four dimensions: introduction likelihood, conditional onward-transmission likelihood after an importation, impact if detection was delayed, and confidence in the judgement. Categories were comparative reference classes rather than calibrated probabilities. Boundary ratings such as 'very low to low' were retained when the evidence did not support a more precise classification."
[^bouhentala-2026-2-methods-10]: [Bouhentala & Kadir (2026) "2. Methods"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.07.19.26358411): "The ratings were calibrated against WHO's 6 June assessment, which classified risk as very high in the DRC, high in Uganda and bordering countries, low in the remainder of the WHO African Region, and low globally."
[^bouhentala-2026-2-methods-13]: [Bouhentala & Kadir (2026) "2. Methods"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.07.19.26358411): "These intervals describe counting variation only and do not incorporate systematic uncertainty from backlogs, under-ascertainment, changing testing, or reporting delays."
[^bouhentala-2026-2-methods-14]: [Bouhentala & Kadir (2026) "2. Methods"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.07.19.26358411): "Risk was separated into four dimensions: introduction likelihood, conditional onward-transmission likelihood after an importation, impact if detection was delayed, and confidence in the judgement. Categories were comparative reference classes rather than calibrated probabilities."
[^bouhentala-2026-3-results-12]: [Bouhentala & Kadir (2026) "3. Results"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.07.19.26358411): "Passenger-volume and confidential readiness data were unavailable."
[^bouhentala-2026-4-discussion-1]: [Bouhentala & Kadir (2026) "4. [^bouhentala-2026-4-discussion-2]: [Bouhentala & Kadir (2026) "4. Discussion"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.07.19.26358411): "An incubating traveller can cross a border while asymptomatic because people are not considered infectious before symptom onset and the incubation period can extend to 21 days. [^bouhentala-2026-3-results-13]: [Bouhentala & Kadir (2026) "3. Results"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.07.19.26358411): "A final departure airport may conceal earlier travel to eastern DRC; triage therefore requires a complete 21-day itinerary and exposure history."
[^bouhentala-2026-2-methods-15]: [Bouhentala & Kadir (2026) "2. Methods"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.07.19.26358411): "Arithmetic cumulative doubling times were treated as descriptive properties of the cumulative curve and not as evidence of changes in transmission."
[^bouhentala-2026-4-discussion-5]: [Bouhentala & Kadir (2026) "4. Discussion"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.07.19.26358411): "Nevertheless, notification rates remain composite measures of infection, care seeking, alert investigation, specimen transport, laboratory capacity, retrospective death investigation, and reporting.
