[^bouhentala-2026-3-results-14]: [Bouhentala & Kadir (2026) "3. Results"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.07.19.26358411): "This rise is compatible with maturation of outcomes among previously active cases, retrospective confirmation of deaths, delayed presentation, preferential detection of severe disease, delayed recovery reporting, and changes in testing or death investigation. It should not be interpreted as a time-resolved measure of clinical virulence."
[^bouhentala-2026-3-results-15]: [Bouhentala & Kadir (2026) "3. Results"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.07.19.26358411): "The published status totals did not fully reconcile: 828 deaths, 390 recoveries, and 725 hospitalised patients summed to 1,943, leaving 181 of 2,124 confirmed cases outside those categories. Public reports did not define the status of these cases. Potential explanations include pending admission or outcome reconciliation, transfers, and reporting lags, but no category could be assigned without line-list data."
[^bouhentala-2026-4-discussion-6]: [Bouhentala & Kadir (2026) "4. Discussion"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.07.19.26358411): "The analysis was time-stamped and may be superseded by subsequent official reports."
[^bouhentala-2026-abstract-3]: [Bouhentala & Kadir (2026) "Abstract"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.07.19.26358411): "** Public surveillance reports from the World Health Organization (WHO), European Centre for Disease Prevention and Control (ECDC), Africa CDC, national ministries of health, and peer-reviewed sources were synthesised through 15–17 July 2026."
[^bouhentala-2026-2-methods-11]: [Bouhentala & Kadir (2026) "2. Methods"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.07.19.26358411): "The main DRC trajectory used laboratory-confirmed cases and deaths among confirmed cases. Suspected, probable, and confirmed categories were not summed across reports because individuals and deaths can be reclassified as investigations progress."
[^bouhentala-2026-3-results-7]: [Bouhentala & Kadir (2026) "3. Results"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.07.19.26358411): "As of 17 July, Uganda's official dashboard reported 20 confirmed cases, two confirmed deaths, 18 recoveries, and no current admissions."
[^bouhentala-2026-3-results-8]: [Bouhentala & Kadir (2026) "3. Results"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.07.19.26358411): "WHO's jurisdictional accounting on 17 July comprised 2,145 confirmed cases globally: 2,124 in the DRC, 20 in Uganda, and one in France, with 830 confirmed-case deaths."
[^bouhentala-2026-2-methods-12]: [Bouhentala & Kadir (2026) "2. Methods"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.07.19.26358411): "The principal sources were WHO Disease Outbreak News, WHO/IHR statements and technical guidance, ECDC surveillance and modelling reports, WHO regional offices, national ministry-of-health dashboards and updates, and peer-reviewed primary studies for historical BDBV outbreaks, viral persistence, and candidate countermeasures."
[^bouhentala-2026-3-results-9]: [Bouhentala & Kadir (2026) "3. Results"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.07.19.26358411): "ECDC estimated approximately one importation per 23,000–24,000 travellers from the principal outbreak region, with a 90% uncertainty interval of one per 13,000–54,000."
[^bouhentala-2026-3-results-10]: [Bouhentala & Kadir (2026) "3. Results"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.07.19.26358411): "These were separately generated Monte Carlo summaries and should not be forced into exact reciprocal equivalence."
[^gashema-2026-1]: [Gashema & Muvunyi (2026)](https://www.thelancet.com/journals/laninf/article/PIIS1473-3099(26)00352-X/fulltext) "A modelling study by Dick Chamla and colleagues, published in *The Lancet Infectious Diseases* shows how fitted stochastic susceptible, exposed, infectious, recovered and deceased (SEIRD) ensemble modelling can leverage limited data to predict cross-border outbreak spread and inform regional preparedness."
[^gashema-2026-2]: [Gashema & Muvunyi (2026)](https://www.thelancet.com/journals/laninf/article/PIIS1473-3099(26)00352-X/fulltext) "Considering epidemiological and contextual factors, the study estimated a high probability of regional spillover to Uganda (94·2%) and South Sudan (69·3%), identifying these countries as priority areas for preparedness. By contrast, the projected risks for Rwanda (8·6%) and Burundi (2·0%) remain comparatively low."
[^gashema-2026-3]: [Gashema & Muvunyi (2026)](https://www.thelancet.com/journals/laninf/article/PIIS1473-3099(26)00352-X/fulltext) "Linking outbreak dynamics with spillover risk, Chamla and colleagues move beyond forecasting disease burden toward informing operational preparedness."
[^gashema-2026-7]: [Gashema & Muvunyi (2026)](https://www.thelancet.com/journals/laninf/article/PIIS1473-3099(26)00352-X/fulltext) "Stochastic models assume homogeneous population mixing and uniform intervention effectiveness, which potentially overlooking important spatial, health-care, and socioeconomic heterogeneities that shape transmission dynamics."
[^gashema-2026-6]: [Gashema & Muvunyi (2026)](https://www.thelancet.com/journals/laninf/article/PIIS1473-3099(26)00352-X/fulltext) "Projections are further constrained by the quality of early surveillance data and approximated mobility patterns, particularly in low or limited resource settings where data gaps are common."
[^gashema-2026-8]: [Gashema & Muvunyi (2026)](https://www.thelancet.com/journals/laninf/article/PIIS1473-3099(26)00352-X/fulltext) "Additionally, these models remain restricted in their ability to capture behavioural adaptation and community engagement, including changes in risk perception, health-care seeking behaviour, intervention uptake, and public trust, all of which can substantially influence outbreak trajectories."
[^gashema-2026-9]: [Gashema & Muvunyi (2026)](https://www.thelancet.com/journals/laninf/article/PIIS1473-3099(26)00352-X/fulltext) "The estimated 6-week interval between the probable index case and formal outbreak declaration created a window during which transmission chains expanded undetected."
[^gashema-2026-10]: [Gashema & Muvunyi (2026)](https://www.thelancet.com/journals/laninf/article/PIIS1473-3099(26)00352-X/fulltext) "The study also demonstrates the limitations of real-time epidemic modelling during rapidly evolving outbreaks. Models developed during unfolding outbreaks inevitably operate under conditions of incomplete information, evolving surveillance systems, and changing response capacities which can affect their ability to accurately predict transmission dynamics. Accordingly, these models are not intended to provide a precise forecast, but rather to identify reasonable transmission trajectories and inform preparedness decisions."
[^gashema-2026-11]: [Gashema & Muvunyi (2026)](https://www.thelancet.com/journals/laninf/article/PIIS1473-3099(26)00352-X/fulltext) "Collectively, these limitations underscore the importance of interpreting model outputs as scenario-based estimates that require continuous refinement as more comprehensive epidemiological, mobility, and behavioural data become available."
[^gashema-2026-5]: [Gashema & Muvunyi (2026)](https://www.thelancet.com/journals/laninf/article/PIIS1473-3099(26)00352-X/fulltext) "The study also underscores the growing importance of epidemic intelligence systems that integrate epidemiological, laboratory, and mobility data into real-time risk assessments."
[^chamla-2026-summary-1]: [Chamla et al. (2026) "Summary"](https://www.thelancet.com/journals/laninf/article/PIIS1473-3099(26)00320-8/fulltext): "The study aimed to establish a short-term trajectory of the BDBV outbreak and probability of cross-border spillover into countries with elevated risk of importation to guide preparedness priorities. We calibrated a stochastic SEIRD (susceptible, exposed, infectious, recovered, dead) ensemble model to the laboratory-confirmed case series, anchoring on 598 cumulative confirmed cases on 8 June 2026 (day 68) using simulation filtering (calibration window plus or minus 30%; reporting fraction 1.0 for laboratory-confirmed cases)."
[^chamla-2026-summary-2]: [Chamla et al. (2026) "Summary"](https://www.thelancet.com/journals/laninf/article/PIIS1473-3099(26)00320-8/fulltext): "A linked daily-hazard spillover model estimated importation probability for Uganda, South Sudan, Rwanda and Burundi over a 12-week horizon."
[^chamla-2026-summary-3]: [Chamla et al. (2026) "Summary"](https://www.thelancet.com/journals/laninf/article/PIIS1473-3099(26)00320-8/fulltext): "Calibrated to the confirmed-case anchor (598 on 8 June 2026; central basic reproduction number [R0] = 1.71), the confirmed-case trajectory is most consistent with the central scenario. Under the central scenario the ensemble projected a median of 990 cumulative confirmed cases by week 12 (24 June 2026; 90% prediction interval [PI] 709 to 1,293) and 174 deaths; the low scenario projected 870 confirmed cases (90% PI 641 to 1,133) and 160 deaths."
[^chamla-2026-results-2]: [Chamla et al. (2026) "Results"](https://www.thelancet.com/journals/laninf/article/PIIS1473-3099(26)00320-8/fulltext): "Under the central transmissibility scenario (mean accepted R0 = 1.71), the ensemble calibrated to the confirmed-case anchor projected a median of 648 cumulative confirmed cases by week 10 (8 June 2026; 50% prediction interval [PI] 560 to 732), rising to 990 by week 12 (24 June 2026; 50% PI 858 to 1,141 and 90% PI 709 to 1,293), with a projected median of 174 deaths at week 12 (Table 1)."
[^chamla-2026-results-3]: [Chamla et al. (2026) "Results"](https://www.thelancet.com/journals/laninf/article/PIIS1473-3099(26)00320-8/fulltext): "By week 24, the central scenario projected a median of 8,210 cumulative confirmed cases (50% PI 6,636 to 10,287 and 90% PI 5,063 to 13,498) and approximately 1,440 deaths if transmission were merely sustained."
[^chamla-2026-results-4]: [Chamla et al. (2026) "Results"](https://www.thelancet.com/journals/laninf/article/PIIS1473-3099(26)00320-8/fulltext): "Under the low-transmissibility scenario (mean accepted R0 = 1.42), cumulative confirmed cases reached a median of 870 by week 12 (50% PI 752 to 1,004 and 90% PI 641 to 1,133; 160 deaths) and 3,662 by week 24 (50% PI 2,884 to 4,484 and 90% PI 2,094 to 6,037; 673 deaths; appendix 3 p 10)."
[^chamla-2026-results-5]: [Chamla et al. (2026) "Results"](https://www.thelancet.com/journals/laninf/article/PIIS1473-3099(26)00320-8/fulltext): "Under the high-transmissibility scenario (mean accepted R0 = 2.08), confirmed cases reached a median of 1,364 (50% PI 1,154 to 1,579 and 90% PI 975 to 1,807; 202 deaths) by week 12, rising to 66,420 (50% PI 56,560 to 78,937 and 90% PI 41,718 to 95,881; 10,644 deaths) by week 24 (appendix 3 p 9)."
[^chamla-2026-results-6]: [Chamla et al. (2026) "Results"](https://www.thelancet.com/journals/laninf/article/PIIS1473-3099(26)00320-8/fulltext): "Because the observed confirmed-case trajectory is consistent with the central scenario, the high scenario is not the operative trajectory; it is retained as an upper-bound contingency should control measures lapse."
[^chamla-2026-methods-3]: [Chamla et al. (2026) "Methods"](https://www.thelancet.com/journals/laninf/article/PIIS1473-3099(26)00320-8/fulltext): "We conducted a stochastic modelling study using a discrete-time SEIRD (susceptible, exposed, infectious, recovered, dead) framework, recalibrated to cumulative reported cases in Ituri Province on 18 May 2026 (day 48) and projected forward over a 24-week horizon to 16 September 2026."
[^chamla-2026-methods-4]: [Chamla et al. (2026) "Methods"](https://www.thelancet.com/journals/laninf/article/PIIS1473-3099(26)00320-8/fulltext): "Daily transitions were implemented as binomial draws using standard mass-action incidence (full mathematical specification in appendix 3 p 2). No vaccinated compartment was included, as there is no licensed BDBV-specific vaccine."
[^chamla-2026-methods-5]: [Chamla et al. (2026) "Methods"](https://www.thelancet.com/journals/laninf/article/PIIS1473-3099(26)00320-8/fulltext): "Three transmissibility scenarios (low basic reproduction number [R0] = 1.42; central R0 = 1.71; high R0 = 2.08) spanned published BDBV estimates."
[^chamla-2026-methods-6]: [Chamla et al. (2026) "Methods"](https://www.thelancet.com/journals/laninf/article/PIIS1473-3099(26)00320-8/fulltext): "Response-intensity reductions applied from 15 May 2026 were 20% (low), 25% (central) and 15% (high)."
[^chamla-2026-methods-7]: [Chamla et al. (2026) "Methods"](https://www.thelancet.com/journals/laninf/article/PIIS1473-3099(26)00320-8/fulltext): "Calibration was performed by simulation filtering (approximate rejection sampling; not formal Bayesian inference). Replicates were accepted if cumulative laboratory-confirmed cases at day 68 (8 June 2026) fell within plus or minus 30% of 598 (range 419 to 777). We retained 400 calibration-passing replicates per scenario."
[^chamla-2026-methods-8]: [Chamla et al. (2026) "Methods"](https://www.thelancet.com/journals/laninf/article/PIIS1473-3099(26)00320-8/fulltext): "Importation risk to Uganda, South Sudan, Rwanda and Burundi was modelled as a daily Poisson hazard driven by calibrated infectious prevalence, country-specific cross-border mobility, point-of-entry detection sensitivity (from WHO Joint External Evaluation assessments) and travel-while-infectious parameters (Uganda 12%; South Sudan 15%; Rwanda and Burundi 8%). Full equations and parameter values are in appendix 3 (pp 2–3)."
[^chamla-2026-methods-9]: [Chamla et al. (2026) "Methods"](https://www.thelancet.com/journals/laninf/article/PIIS1473-3099(26)00320-8/fulltext): "Importation probability = 1 − exp(cumulative 12-week hazard). Probabilities of at least one importation were estimated for Uganda, South Sudan, Rwanda and Burundi."
[^chamla-2026-methods-10]: [Chamla et al. (2026) "Methods"](https://www.thelancet.com/journals/laninf/article/PIIS1473-3099(26)00320-8/fulltext): "Sensitivity analyses examined travel-while-infectious (5 to 25%), reporting completeness (0.60 to 0.90) and catchment size (250,000 to 500,000)."
[^chamla-2026-methods-12]: [Chamla et al. (2026) "Methods"](https://www.thelancet.com/journals/laninf/article/PIIS1473-3099(26)00320-8/fulltext): "These distributions were chosen to span the range of published BDBV estimates from the 2007 Uganda and 2012 DR Congo outbreaks."
[^chamla-2026-summary-8]: [Chamla et al. (2026) "Summary"](https://www.thelancet.com/journals/laninf/article/PIIS1473-3099(26)00320-8/fulltext): "Calibrated to the confirmed-case anchor (598 on 8 June 2026; central basic reproduction number [R0] = 1.71), the confirmed-case trajectory is most consistent with the central scenario."
[^chamla-2026-methods-13]: [Chamla et al. (2026) "Methods"](https://www.thelancet.com/journals/laninf/article/PIIS1473-3099(26)00320-8/fulltext): "The latent period was fixed at 9.7 days and the infectious period at 5.0 days, in line with the broader filovirus literature."
[^chamla-2026-methods-14]: [Chamla et al. (2026) "Methods"](https://www.thelancet.com/journals/laninf/article/PIIS1473-3099(26)00320-8/fulltext): "The case-fatality ratio was drawn from a uniform distribution (0.15 to 0.25; appendix 3 pp 3–4), centred on the case-fatality ratio observed among laboratory-confirmed cases in the current outbreak (115 [approximately 19%] of 598 as of 8 June 2026) and consistent with the historical BDBV range."
[^chamla-2026-methods-16]: [Chamla et al. (2026) "Methods"](https://www.thelancet.com/journals/laninf/article/PIIS1473-3099(26)00320-8/fulltext): "Because the analysis is anchored on laboratory-confirmed cases, the reporting fraction was set at 1.0 (laboratory-confirmed cases are, by definition, ascertained); the earlier suspected-case reporting fraction assumption is no longer required."
[^chamla-2026-methods-17]: [Chamla et al. (2026) "Methods"](https://www.thelancet.com/journals/laninf/article/PIIS1473-3099(26)00320-8/fulltext): "The model incorporated a transmission reduction factor representing the combined effects of infection prevention and control measures, community engagement, safe and dignified burial and contact tracing, with reduction factors varying by scenario to reflect differing operational effectiveness from the date of outbreak declaration onward."
[^chamla-2026-discussion-1]: [Chamla et al. (2026) "Discussion"](https://www.thelancet.com/journals/laninf/article/PIIS1473-3099(26)00320-8/fulltext): "The response-intensity factor was applied uniformly from 15 May, but real-world response effectiveness varies by place, time, access and community acceptance."
[^chamla-2026-methods-18]: [Chamla et al. (2026) "Methods"](https://www.thelancet.com/journals/laninf/article/PIIS1473-3099(26)00320-8/fulltext): "We modelled transmission within a homogeneously mixing catchment of 350,000 (seven affected health zones, including Ituri and North Kivu). Spatial heterogeneity is not captured; this is a stated limitation."
[^chamla-2026-methods-19]: [Chamla et al. (2026) "Methods"](https://www.thelancet.com/journals/laninf/article/PIIS1473-3099(26)00320-8/fulltext): "Calibration was performed by simulation filtering (approximate rejection sampling; not formal Bayesian inference)."
[^chamla-2026-methods-20]: [Chamla et al. (2026) "Methods"](https://www.thelancet.com/journals/laninf/article/PIIS1473-3099(26)00320-8/fulltext): "The plus or minus 30% tolerance was chosen pragmatically given residual data uncertainty."
[^chamla-2026-discussion-2]: [Chamla et al. (2026) "Discussion"](https://www.thelancet.com/journals/laninf/article/PIIS1473-3099(26)00320-8/fulltext): "Homogeneous mixing oversimplifies spatial heterogeneity across health zones; healthcare-worker transmission was not separately modelled; response intensity was applied uniformly; the model is anchored on a single recent confirmed-case datum (598 on 8 June 2026), so the fit is sensitive to that anchor; simulation-filtering is not formal inference (appendix 3 p 6); and informal cross-border mobility estimates are imprecise."
[^chamla-2026-discussion-3]: [Chamla et al. (2026) "Discussion"](https://www.thelancet.com/journals/laninf/article/PIIS1473-3099(26)00320-8/fulltext): "The response-intensity factor was applied uniformly from 15 May, but real-world response effectiveness varies by place, time, access and community acceptance. Live mobility data and behavioural adaptation were not incorporated."
[^chamla-2026-discussion-4]: [Chamla et al. (2026) "Discussion"](https://www.thelancet.com/journals/laninf/article/PIIS1473-3099(26)00320-8/fulltext): "Surveillance dynamics, particularly the post-declaration case-finding surge, probably contributed to the rapid apparent growth between 15 and 19 May; we addressed this by lowering the reporting fraction and widening the calibration window, but residual confounding between transmission and surveillance intensity remains."
[^chamla-2026-discussion-5]: [Chamla et al. (2026) "Discussion"](https://www.thelancet.com/journals/laninf/article/PIIS1473-3099(26)00320-8/fulltext): "Most importantly, the calibration relied on early-phase surveillance counts captured in the first weekly WHO External Situation Report (data as of 18 May); the cumulative suspected-case figure of 528 was only partially resolved into 35 laboratory-confirmed cases at the time of analysis, and the associated death count of 132 reflected surveillance signals across the affected health zones rather than laboratory-confirmed mortality alone (of which there were five). Misclassification, delayed laboratory throughput and ongoing active case finding could each shift the calibration anchor in either direction."
[^chamla-2026-discussion-7]: [Chamla et al. (2026) "Discussion"](https://www.thelancet.com/journals/laninf/article/PIIS1473-3099(26)00320-8/fulltext): "The projections presented here should therefore be interpreted as an early situational assessment rather than a definitive forecast and will be updated as verified line-list data become available."
[^chamla-2026-discussion-8]: [Chamla et al. (2026) "Discussion"](https://www.thelancet.com/journals/laninf/article/PIIS1473-3099(26)00320-8/fulltext): "Individual-level sex and gender data were not available in the aggregate surveillance reports used for this modelling study; disaggregated analysis by sex or gender was therefore not possible."
[^chamla-2026-methods-11]: [Chamla et al. (2026) "Methods"](https://www.thelancet.com/journals/laninf/article/PIIS1473-3099(26)00320-8/fulltext): "Case data were taken from the WHO AFRO External Situation Reports and the WHO AFRO internal tracker through to 8 June 2026. The laboratory-confirmed case series was used as the primary calibration metric: confirmed cases rose from 33 on 18 May 2026 to 105 on 24 May 2026, and 598 on 8 June 2026."
[^chamla-2026-summary-7]: [Chamla et al. (2026) "Summary"](https://www.thelancet.com/journals/laninf/article/PIIS1473-3099(26)00320-8/fulltext): "Uganda reported seven confirmed cases (three imported; four locally acquired including three healthcare workers; case-fatality ratio 14%)."
[^chamla-2026-results-7]: [Chamla et al. (2026) "Results"](https://www.thelancet.com/journals/laninf/article/PIIS1473-3099(26)00320-8/fulltext): "Cross-border spillover projections (Figure 3 and Table 2) indicated very high importation probability for Uganda (94.2%; expected imports 2.85), where the outbreak is now firmly established: as of 4 June 2026, Uganda reported 19 confirmed cases (including five healthcare-worker infections and two deaths), reflecting sustained importation and onward local transmission since the first imported case."
[^richard-2026-abstract-1]: [Richard et al. (2026) "Abstract"](https://www.cdc.gov/mmwr/volumes/75/wr/mm7522e2.htm): "In response to reports of high numbers of suspected cases and deaths in the affected countries, CDC assessed the risk posed by the BVD outbreak to the U.S. population during the next 3 months. This analysis used a standardized risk assessment approach that included epidemiologic data from the ongoing outbreak and historical data from previous Ebola outbreaks; the overall risk was determined by taking into account independent assessments of the likelihood of infection and the impact of infection."
[^richard-2026-methods-1]: [Richard et al. (2026) "Methods"](https://www.cdc.gov/mmwr/volumes/75/wr/mm7522e2.htm): "Overall risk to the U.S. population was determined by independently assessing two factors: (1) the likelihood of infection and (2) the impact of infection."
[^richard-2026-results-2]: [Richard et al. (2026) "Results"](https://www.cdc.gov/mmwr/volumes/75/wr/mm7522e2.htm): "CDC assessed the overall risk posed by the ongoing BVD outbreak to the U.S. population during the next 3 months as low. This assessment was made with moderate confidence, given the data available."
[^richard-2026-results-3]: [Richard et al. (2026) "Results"](https://www.cdc.gov/mmwr/volumes/75/wr/mm7522e2.htm): "This overall risk was determined based on a combination of extremely low likelihood of infection, but high impact of infection for the U.S. population, were it to occur. The likelihood of Bundibugyo virus infection for the U.S. population was assessed as extremely low."
[^richard-2026-results-4]: [Richard et al. (2026) "Results"](https://www.cdc.gov/mmwr/volumes/75/wr/mm7522e2.htm): "These modeling results suggested that the relative risk of importation to the United States compared with other locations was 1.3%."
[^richard-2026-results-5]: [Richard et al. (2026) "Results"](https://www.cdc.gov/mmwr/volumes/75/wr/mm7522e2.htm): "The impact of infection, based on the standardized framework, was assessed as high, primarily based on the severity of the illness, lack of available medications and vaccines, and resources required to respond to the current outbreak. In the two previously identified outbreaks of BVD in Uganda in 2007 and DRC in 2012, case-fatality rates ranged from 25% to 50%."
[^richard-2026-methods-2]: [Richard et al. (2026) "Methods"](https://www.cdc.gov/mmwr/volumes/75/wr/mm7522e2.htm): "CDC subject matter experts in risk assessment methodology, infectious disease modeling, global health, and Ebola disease and viral hemorrhagic fevers collaborated to develop this assessment. These experts used a standardized risk assessment approach that has been applied to previous viral hemorrhagic fever outbreaks. They considered available evidence including epidemiologic data from the ongoing BVD outbreak and historical data on Bundibugyo virus and other Ebola disease outbreaks."
[^richard-2026-methods-3]: [Richard et al. (2026) "Methods"](https://www.cdc.gov/mmwr/volumes/75/wr/mm7522e2.htm): "Overall risk to the U.S. population was determined by independently assessing two factors: (1) the likelihood of infection and (2) the impact of infection. † The likelihood of infection refers to the probability that members of the U.S. population would acquire Bundibugyo virus infection during the next 3 months; this, in turn, depends on the likelihood of exposure, infectiousness of the virus, and susceptibility of the population. The impact of infection refers to the consequences of infection in this population. Factors include the severity of disease, level of population immunity to severe disease, availability of medications and vaccines, and necessary public health response resources."
[^richard-2026-methods-4]: [Richard et al. (2026) "Methods"](https://www.cdc.gov/mmwr/volumes/75/wr/mm7522e2.htm): "Experts then assigned a degree of confidence to the assessment, taking into account evidence quality, extent, and corroboration of information."
[^richard-2026-results-1]: [Richard et al. (2026) "Results"](https://www.cdc.gov/mmwr/volumes/75/wr/mm7522e2.htm): "Despite the large number of cases identified at the time the outbreak was reported, the current likelihood for potential spread of BVD from DRC to the United States, via travelers from DRC who might be infected, is considered very low based on modeling results that consider population movement. These modeling results suggested that the relative risk of importation to the United States compared with other locations was 1.3%."
[^richard-2026-results-8]: [Richard et al. (2026) "Results"](https://www.cdc.gov/mmwr/volumes/75/wr/mm7522e2.htm): "Despite the large number of cases identified at the time the outbreak was reported, the current likelihood for potential spread of BVD from DRC to the United States, via travelers from DRC who might be infected, is considered very low based on modeling results that consider population movement."
[^richard-2026-results-9]: [Richard et al. (2026) "Results"](https://www.cdc.gov/mmwr/volumes/75/wr/mm7522e2.htm): "If BVD were to be introduced into the United States, based on historical observation and the known epidemiology of BVD, secondary transmission would likely be minimal."
[^richard-2026-results-10]: [Richard et al. (2026) "Results"](https://www.cdc.gov/mmwr/volumes/75/wr/mm7522e2.htm): "Although BVD symptoms can appear suddenly and might be nonspecific, these public health measures are highly effective against Ebola disease, in part because the average interval between cases is long (10–16 days), and because persons are not known to be infectious before the onset of symptoms."
[^richard-2026-methods-6]: [Richard et al. (2026) "Methods"](https://www.cdc.gov/mmwr/volumes/75/wr/mm7522e2.htm): "Risk was assessed only for the general U.S. population; however, subpopulations that might have different assessments of likelihood are noted, based on varying risk factors."
[^richard-2026-results-11]: [Richard et al. (2026) "Results"](https://www.cdc.gov/mmwr/volumes/75/wr/mm7522e2.htm): "Although the likelihood of infection for the general U.S. population is low, the likelihood of infection might be higher among U.S. health care workers practicing in or who have recently returned from affected regions in DRC and Uganda based on possible exposure risks."
[^richard-2026-results-12]: [Richard et al. (2026) "Results"](https://www.cdc.gov/mmwr/volumes/75/wr/mm7522e2.htm): "In the two previously identified outbreaks of BVD in Uganda in 2007 and DRC in 2012, case-fatality rates ranged from 25% to 50%. However, many of the deaths in these outbreaks occurred in locations where health resources are limited; clinical outcomes might improve with the specialized care available in the United States."
[^richard-2026-results-13]: [Richard et al. (2026) "Results"](https://www.cdc.gov/mmwr/volumes/75/wr/mm7522e2.htm): "However, this assessment also recognizes uncertainties about the epidemiology of BVD, the scope and geographic spread of the outbreak, and the potential timelines for implementation of interventions."
[^richard-2026-discussion-1]: [Richard et al. (2026) "Discussion"](https://www.cdc.gov/mmwr/volumes/75/wr/mm7522e2.htm): "First, given that only two previous BVD outbreaks have occurred, less is known about Bundibugyo virus than other types of orthoebolaviruses that cause human illness (e.g., *O. zairense*, which was responsible for the 2014–2016 Ebola virus disease outbreak in West Africa)."
[^richard-2026-discussion-2]: [Richard et al. (2026) "Discussion"](https://www.cdc.gov/mmwr/volumes/75/wr/mm7522e2.htm): "Second, limited confirmatory diagnostic testing and challenges in contact tracing that likely resulted in undetected transmission mean that the full scope of the current outbreak is unclear, making it difficult to assess the potential geographic spread of the outbreak and the subsequent potential for spread to the United States."
[^richard-2026-discussion-3]: [Richard et al. (2026) "Discussion"](https://www.cdc.gov/mmwr/volumes/75/wr/mm7522e2.htm): "Finally, although DRC and neighboring countries have extensive experience responding to outbreaks of viral hemorrhagic fevers, several features of this outbreak pose challenges to assessing its future trajectory, including that the outbreak is occurring in a region with ongoing conflict and unpredictable infrastructure, and the initial large size of the outbreak combined with limited confirmatory diagnostic testing might lead to challenges in infection prevention and control efforts."
[^richard-2026-abstract-4]: [Richard et al. (2026) "Abstract"](https://www.cdc.gov/mmwr/volumes/75/wr/mm7522e2.htm): "Limitations to this assessment included uncertainties around the epidemiology of BVD as well as the current and future scope and geographic spread of the outbreak."
[^richard-2026-introduction-1]: [Richard et al. (2026) "Introduction"](https://www.cdc.gov/mmwr/volumes/75/wr/mm7522e2.htm): "As of June 2, 2026, a total of 378 confirmed BVD cases and 63 deaths have been reported in the Democratic Republic of the Congo (DRC) and Uganda."
[^richard-2026-methods-5]: [Richard et al. (2026) "Methods"](https://www.cdc.gov/mmwr/volumes/75/wr/mm7522e2.htm): "They considered available evidence including epidemiologic data from the ongoing BVD outbreak and historical data on Bundibugyo virus and other Ebola disease outbreaks."
[^richard-2026-results-6]: [Richard et al. (2026) "Results"](https://www.cdc.gov/mmwr/volumes/75/wr/mm7522e2.htm): "In the two previously identified outbreaks of BVD in Uganda in 2007 and DRC in 2012, case-fatality rates ranged from 25% to 50%."
[^richard-2026-results-7]: [Richard et al. (2026) "Results"](https://www.cdc.gov/mmwr/volumes/75/wr/mm7522e2.htm): "Only 11 persons infected with Ebola disease have ever been treated in the United States; all were associated with the 2014–2016 Ebola virus disease outbreak in West Africa. Despite two instances of secondary transmission to U.S. health care workers during that outbreak, no community spread occurred in the United States."
[^van-zandvoort-2026-ebola-disease-cases-outside-africa-1]: [van Zandvoort et al. (2026) "Ebola disease cases outside Africa"](https://www.eurosurveillance.org/content/10.2807/1560-7917.ES.2026.31.24.2600508): "We conducted internet searches for all laboratory-confirmed Ebola disease cases presenting outside Africa, performing both manual and artificial intelligence (AI)-assisted internet searches to identify scientific articles, public health bulletins and news reports without any time limit."
[^van-zandvoort-2026-summary-1]: [van Zandvoort et al. (2026) "Summary"](https://www.eurosurveillance.org/content/10.2807/1560-7917.ES.2026.31.24.2600508): "Following the Bundibugyo virus disease outbreak reported in the Democratic Republic of the Congo in May 2026, we reviewed all known Ebola disease cases outside Africa and found that intercontinental transmission risk remains low."
[^van-zandvoort-2026-low-overall-risk-of-exportation-2]: [van Zandvoort et al. (2026) "Low overall risk of exportation"](https://www.eurosurveillance.org/content/10.2807/1560-7917.ES.2026.31.24.2600508): "This gives a crude risk estimate of 0.81 cases outside Africa, including medically evacuated, latent and secondary cases, per 1,000 Ebola disease cases in a source outbreak (Table 2). Focusing on latent cases and their secondary cases only, there have been 0.17 cases outside Africa per 1,000 Ebola disease cases in the source outbreak."
[^van-zandvoort-2026-low-overall-risk-of-exportation-3]: [van Zandvoort et al. (2026) "Low overall risk of exportation"](https://www.eurosurveillance.org/content/10.2807/1560-7917.ES.2026.31.24.2600508): "Of note, this represents the empirically observed risk of importation (and onward transmission) across all countries outside Africa; per-country risks are smaller. For example, focusing only on the UK, there has been one latent Ebola disease case since 2000, giving a risk estimate of 0.03 latent UK cases for every 1,000 Ebola disease cases in a source outbreak."
[^van-zandvoort-2026-decrease-in-exportation-risk-over-the-3]: [van Zandvoort et al. (2026) "Decrease in exportation risk over the 2014–16 epidemic"](https://www.eurosurveillance.org/content/10.2807/1560-7917.ES.2026.31.24.2600508): "Over the course of the epidemic, the case exportation risk per reported source outbreak case decreased by 4.6% per week (95% CI: 1.7–7.4%; p = 0.0097). More generally, the case exportation risk was substantially lower after the epidemic peak (0.46; 95% CI: 0.17–0.73 exported cases per 1,000 source cases from 27 October 2014 onwards) than it was before the peak (1.7; 95% CI: 0.95–2.7 exported cases per 1,000 source cases)."
[^van-zandvoort-2026-summary-3]: [van Zandvoort et al. (2026) "Summary"](https://www.eurosurveillance.org/content/10.2807/1560-7917.ES.2026.31.24.2600508): "We identified and analysed all known Ebola disease cases outside Africa to contextualise intercontinental spread and assess the risk of undetected *Orthoebolavirus* transmission outside Africa. We conducted internet searches for all laboratory-confirmed Ebola disease cases presenting outside Africa, performing both manual and artificial intelligence (AI)-assisted internet searches to identify scientific articles, public health bulletins and news reports without any time limit."
[^van-zandvoort-2026-low-overall-risk-of-exportation-1]: [van Zandvoort et al. (2026) "Low overall risk of exportation"](https://www.eurosurveillance.org/content/10.2807/1560-7917.ES.2026.31.24.2600508): "As a crude measure of risk, we calculated for Ebola disease outbreaks since the year 2000 the number of cases occurring outside Africa per 1,000 reported cases in the originating epidemic. This gives a crude risk estimate of 0.81 cases outside Africa, including medically evacuated, latent and secondary cases, per 1,000 Ebola disease cases in a source outbreak (Table 2). Focusing on latent cases and their secondary cases only, there have been 0.17 cases outside Africa per 1,000 Ebola disease cases in the source outbreak."
[^van-zandvoort-2026-decrease-in-exportation-risk-over-the-1]: [van Zandvoort et al. (2026) "Decrease in exportation risk over the 2014–16 epidemic"](https://www.eurosurveillance.org/content/10.2807/1560-7917.ES.2026.31.24.2600508): "For cases exported from the 2014–16 epidemic in Western Africa, we carried out a time-varying risk analysis using Poisson regression of the number of exported cases for a given epidemiological week and country (across Guinea, Liberia and Sierra Leone, including weeks with no exported cases, from 6 January 2014 to 20 December 2015) against (i) the number of cases reported for that epidemiological week and country and (ii) the number of weeks since 6 January 2014 as a linear predictor of time-varying risk."
[^van-zandvoort-2026-decrease-in-exportation-risk-over-the-2]: [van Zandvoort et al. (2026) "Decrease in exportation risk over the 2014–16 epidemic"](https://www.eurosurveillance.org/content/10.2807/1560-7917.ES.2026.31.24.2600508): "The number of exported cases (both medically evacuated and latent) closely scaled with the number of cases in the source country in that week (Figure 2), with no significant deviation from linear scaling (estimated scaling: 0.98; 95% CI (confidence interval): 0.60–1.36; p = 0.86)."
[^van-zandvoort-2026-low-overall-risk-of-exportation-4]: [van Zandvoort et al. (2026) "Low overall risk of exportation"](https://www.eurosurveillance.org/content/10.2807/1560-7917.ES.2026.31.24.2600508): "As a crude measure of risk, we calculated for Ebola disease outbreaks since the year 2000 the number of cases occurring outside Africa per 1,000 reported cases in the originating epidemic."
[^van-zandvoort-2026-discussion-2]: [van Zandvoort et al. (2026) "Discussion"](https://www.eurosurveillance.org/content/10.2807/1560-7917.ES.2026.31.24.2600508): "A key distinction in our analysis is between medically evacuated and latent Ebola disease cases. While medically evacuated cases represent known risks that can be mitigated with strict biosecurity measures, latent cases arriving via commercial flights need to be diagnosed before they can be isolated."
[^van-zandvoort-2026-discussion-3]: [van Zandvoort et al. (2026) "Discussion"](https://www.eurosurveillance.org/content/10.2807/1560-7917.ES.2026.31.24.2600508): "Agreement among search methods and with a clinical review of exported cases from 2014 to 2016 support that our list is likely complete. Nonetheless, potential sources of under-ascertainment include that public health bulletins published not in English were underrepresented despite targeted native language searches, and web-based search tools may have returned geographically biased results."
[^van-zandvoort-2026-discussion-4]: [van Zandvoort et al. (2026) "Discussion"](https://www.eurosurveillance.org/content/10.2807/1560-7917.ES.2026.31.24.2600508): "Nonetheless, potential sources of under-ascertainment include that public health bulletins published not in English were underrepresented despite targeted native language searches, and web-based search tools may have returned geographically biased results."
[^van-zandvoort-2026-decrease-in-exportation-risk-over-the-5]: [van Zandvoort et al. (2026) "Decrease in exportation risk over the 2014–16 epidemic"](https://www.eurosurveillance.org/content/10.2807/1560-7917.ES.2026.31.24.2600508): "As most exported primary cases during this epidemic (20 of 24) were medical evacuations, this is likely to be most consistent with improvement in infection prevention and control measures coinciding with the stepping-up of response efforts in late 2014, as further evidenced by decreasing transmission after the peak of the epidemic."
[^van-zandvoort-2026-low-overall-risk-of-exportation-5]: [van Zandvoort et al. (2026) "Low overall risk of exportation"](https://www.eurosurveillance.org/content/10.2807/1560-7917.ES.2026.31.24.2600508): "Of note, this represents the empirically observed risk of importation (and onward transmission) across all countries outside Africa; per-country risks are smaller."
[^van-zandvoort-2026-discussion-5]: [van Zandvoort et al. (2026) "Discussion"](https://www.eurosurveillance.org/content/10.2807/1560-7917.ES.2026.31.24.2600508): "Notably, the four latent cases we identified occurred among 300,000 travellers screened by the US Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC)-supported exit screening programmes in Guinea, Liberia and Sierra Leone, and all were asymptomatic (and hence undetectable) at the point of both exit screening and entry screening. Screening protocols included clear guidance on procedures for self-monitoring of symptoms, which ultimately led to rapid confirmation and isolation among the three latent cases in medical workers."
[^van-zandvoort-2026-ebola-disease-cases-outside-africa-6]: [van Zandvoort et al. (2026) "Ebola disease cases outside Africa"](https://www.eurosurveillance.org/content/10.2807/1560-7917.ES.2026.31.24.2600508): "We identified 28 confirmed Ebola disease cases outside Africa: 25 primary imported cases and three secondary cases infected by another patient in the United States (US) or Europe (Table 1, Figure 1). Of these, 27 occurred during the 2014–16 Ebola virus epidemic in Western Africa and one has occurred so far during the ongoing 2026 Bundibugyo virus epidemic."
[^van-zandvoort-2026-ebola-disease-cases-outside-africa-7]: [van Zandvoort et al. (2026) "Ebola disease cases outside Africa"](https://www.eurosurveillance.org/content/10.2807/1560-7917.ES.2026.31.24.2600508): "In total, we included 27 scientific articles, 9 reports in public health bulletins, 82 news articles and 9 other sites in gathering data."
[^van-zandvoort-2026-decrease-in-exportation-risk-over-the-4]: [van Zandvoort et al. (2026) "Decrease in exportation risk over the 2014–16 epidemic"](https://www.eurosurveillance.org/content/10.2807/1560-7917.ES.2026.31.24.2600508): "Weekly count of new confirmed, probable and suspected cases, by week and country for Guinea, Liberia and Sierra Leone (total 28,592)."
[^van-zandvoort-2026-discussion-1]: [van Zandvoort et al. (2026) "Discussion"](https://www.eurosurveillance.org/content/10.2807/1560-7917.ES.2026.31.24.2600508): "Notably, the four latent cases we identified occurred among 300,000 travellers screened by the US Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC)-supported exit screening programmes in Guinea, Liberia and Sierra Leone, and all were asymptomatic (and hence undetectable) at the point of both exit screening and entry screening."
[^van-zandvoort-2026-ebola-disease-cases-outside-africa-8]: [van Zandvoort et al. (2026) "Ebola disease cases outside Africa"](https://www.eurosurveillance.org/content/10.2807/1560-7917.ES.2026.31.24.2600508): "We checked our findings against existing lists of Ebola disease cases."
[^fanelli-2026b-abstract-1]: [Fanelli et al. (2026b) "Abstract"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.05.31.26354511): "Using International Air Transport Association origin-destination passenger flows, we assessed relative exposure to Ebola virus disease importation into Europe under six outbreak expansion scenarios reflecting plausible pathways of geographical spread, including cross-border transmission and amplification in highly connected regional capitals."
[^fanelli-2026b-text-3]: [Fanelli et al. (2026b) "Text"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.05.31.26354511): "Potential spread across the Ugandan border produced no change in either the relative exposure of European countries (Fig. 1b) or their overall importation pressure (Fig. 1c), compared with the currently reported localized transmission in eastern DRC, with Italy, France, Belgium, and the UK being the most exposed."
[^fanelli-2026b-text-4]: [Fanelli et al. (2026b) "Text"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.05.31.26354511): "Expansion across the border to South Sudan produced a first structural increase in importation pressure through the connectivity associated to Juba (5-fold increase for Northern Europe and 6-fold increase for Eastern Europe relative to localized transmission in DRC), while still leaving UK, France, and Italy among the highest-risk countries."
[^fanelli-2026b-appendix-6]: [Fanelli et al. (2026b) "Appendix"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.05.31.26354511): "Results do not show notable differences with those reported in the main text (Figure S2, S3), with the exception of the Kampala amplification scenario, in which the increase in importation pressure for Northern Europe rises to approximately 190-fold when using monthly averages of 2024 travel data."
[^fanelli-2026b-appendix-2]: [Fanelli et al. (2026b) "Appendix"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.05.31.26354511): "We use origin-destination (O&amp;D) passenger flows derived from the 2024 International Air Transport Association (IATA) database to reconstruct intercontinental and regional travel patterns at the airport level. The IATA O&amp;D data capture complete passenger itineraries, including traffic travelling via connecting flights rather than only direct flight segments."
[^fanelli-2026b-appendix-3]: [Fanelli et al. (2026b) "Appendix"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.05.31.26354511): "The relative importation risk to country c was estimated as the proportion of total outgoing passenger flow from the selected seed airports directed to the country, for each scenario: where $w_{ij}$ denotes the origin-destination passenger flow from airport $i$ , among the set $O_S$ of origin airports included in scenario $S$ , and airport $j$ in the set $D_c$ of airports of the destination destination country $c$. The relative risk of importation for each European country was normalized to the total passenger flow from the source airports to Europe."
[^fanelli-2026b-appendix-4]: [Fanelli et al. (2026b) "Appendix"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.05.31.26354511): "Risks were then stratified by subregion."
[^fanelli-2026b-appendix-5]: [Fanelli et al. (2026b) "Appendix"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.05.31.26354511): "To account for potential seasonal effects in travel patterns, we conducted the analysis using average monthly data from the full 2024. Results do not show notable differences with those reported in the main text (Figure S2, S3), with the exception of the Kampala amplification scenario, in which the increase in importation pressure for Northern Europe rises to approximately 190-fold when using monthly averages of 2024 travel data."
[^fanelli-2026b-text-6]: [Fanelli et al. (2026b) "Text"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.05.31.26354511): "We considered six outbreak expansion scenarios designed to reflect plausible pathways of geographical spread from the currently affected area through porous borders (Fig. 1a, Appendix)."
[^fanelli-2026b-appendix-8]: [Fanelli et al. (2026b) "Appendix"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.05.31.26354511): "All analyses presented in this report are based on the cumulative passenger traffic reported for May, June, and July 2024. Data from 2024 showed moderate seasonal variability in passenger traffic to Europe. Relative to the annual traffic to Europe, mean passenger volumes during MayJuly ranged from 3% below to 16% above the monthly 2024 average across scenarios."
[^fanelli-2026b-text-7]: [Fanelli et al. (2026b) "Text"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.05.31.26354511): "Scenarios 4-6 explored hypothetical amplification assuming community transmission in major urban hubs-Kampala, Kigali, and Kinshasa."
[^fanelli-2026b-text-8]: [Fanelli et al. (2026b) "Text"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.05.31.26354511): "Patterns of Ebola virus importation risk to Europe may rapidly shift under uncertainty surrounding the geographical extent and evolution of the outbreak, particularly if transmission expands into major urban hubs with strong international connectivity near the affected area."
[^fanelli-2026b-text-1]: [Fanelli et al. (2026b) "Text"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.05.31.26354511): "As of 29 May 2026, the Bundibugyo Ebola outbreak, a Public Health Emergency of International Concern, involved 125 confirmed cases (17 deaths), and 906 suspected cases (223 deaths) across Ituri, North Kivu, and South Kivu provinces in Eastern DRC, and 9 confirmed cases (1 death) in Uganda."
[^fanelli-2026b-appendix-1]: [Fanelli et al. (2026b) "Appendix"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.05.31.26354511): "All analyses presented in this report are based on the cumulative passenger traffic reported for May, June, and July 2024."
[^fanelli-2026b-appendix-7]: [Fanelli et al. (2026b) "Appendix"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.05.31.26354511): "Relative to the annual traffic to Europe, mean passenger volumes during MayJuly ranged from 3% below to 16% above the monthly 2024 average across scenarios."
[^fanelli-2026a-abstract-1]: [Fanelli et al. (2026a) "ABSTRACT"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.07.21.26358330): "We developed an integrated spatial framework combining high-resolution epidemic and population data, air and land mobility, geographical accessibility, conflict-adjusted travel times to estimate the risk of Ebola importation across African countries. We contextualized importation risk using different national readiness indicators."
[^fanelli-2026a-abstract-2]: [Fanelli et al. (2026a) "ABSTRACT"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.07.21.26358330): "Importation risk was highly concentrated geographically, with neighbouring countries accounting for 85% of the estimated continental risk. However, high-risk destinations extended beyond the immediate neighbours, whereas some neighbouring countries remained at low risk, reflecting the complementary roles of land and air mobility in shaping regional dissemination. South Sudan, Rwanda, Kenya, Tanzania, Zambia, Ethiopia, Burundi, Angola, South Africa, the Central African Republic, and Nigeria were estimated at high risk, but differed markedly in healthcare system capacity and emergency preparedness and response. Readiness gaps were largest in South Sudan, Burundi, and the Central African Republic."
[^fanelli-2026a-results-2]: [Fanelli et al. (2026a) "RESULTS"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.07.21.26358330): "Neighboring countries alone accounted for 85% of the total estimated risk, while countries located two border crossings away contributed an additional 9%, highlighting the strong spatial concentration of potential spread around the outbreak source (**Fig. 2f**)."
[^fanelli-2026a-results-3]: [Fanelli et al. (2026a) "RESULTS"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.07.21.26358330): "Ethiopia, South Africa, and Nigeria were the only non-neighbouring countries classified as high risk overall, and each ranked substantially higher when considering air importation risk than land importation risk: Ethiopia ranked first for air importation risk but fifth for land importation risk, while South Africa ranked third and ninth, respectively, and Nigeria seventh and eleventh."
[^fanelli-2026a-results-5]: [Fanelli et al. (2026a) "RESULTS"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.07.21.26358330): "Epidemic amplification in Nairobi and Lusaka produced the largest increase in continental importation (4-fold increase), and especially in southern Africa (up to 15-fold). Amplification in Kampala and Kigali produced a moderate increase in importation risk across the continent (2.5-fold and 2.8-fold, respectively), whereas expansion to Kinshasa, Bujumbura, or Juba produced comparatively limited continental effects."
[^fanelli-2026a-introduction-2]: [Fanelli et al. (2026a) "INTRODUCTION"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.07.21.26358330): "We developed an integrated analytical framework to estimate the risk of Ebola Bundibugyo dissemination in Africa in relation to national readiness, by combining epidemiological and high-resolution spatial population data, commercial air transportation, land mobility, geographical accessibility, conflict-adjusted travel-times, and different readiness scores."
[^fanelli-2026a-methods-4]: [Fanelli et al. (2026a) "METHODS"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.07.21.26358330): "The importation risk for each African country was estimated separately for air and land mobility, as the probability that an infected traveller from the affected areas would reach that country. We assumed that Ebola infected cases could travel only in their latent phase, as symptomatic patients would be expected to either remain in the affected country for clinical management or, if transferred internationally, travel under medically supervised and controlled conditions. Therefore, for land mobility, travel times were explicitly incorporated to estimate the destination reached before the onset of symptoms. For air travel, journey times were considered negligible relative to the incubation period."
[^fanelli-2026a-methods-5]: [Fanelli et al. (2026a) "METHODS"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.07.21.26358330): "Risks were weighted by the cumulative attack rate in each affected administrative unit. Air and land importation risks were then combined to obtain an overall estimate of relative importation risk for each destination country."
[^fanelli-2026a-methods-6]: [Fanelli et al. (2026a) "METHODS"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.07.21.26358330): "Air mobility was derived from the International Air Transport Association (IATA) data on origin–destination passenger flows between any pair of commercial airports and accounting for connections at intermediate airports (**Fig. 1c**)."
[^fanelli-2026a-methods-7]: [Fanelli et al. (2026a) "METHODS"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.07.21.26358330): "For air travel, it was assigned using weights proportional to population size and decreasing with the distance to each airport of the country."
[^fanelli-2026a-methods-8]: [Fanelli et al. (2026a) "METHODS"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.07.21.26358330): "Land mobility was then obtained as residual mobility by subtracting air mobility from the Global Transnational Mobility Dataset 2.0 (GTMD2), which provides estimates of overall population movements between countries (**Fig. 1d**)."
[^fanelli-2026a-methods-9]: [Fanelli et al. (2026a) "METHODS"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.07.21.26358330): "To characterize spatial accessibility from the outbreak area and the potential for land-based spread within the incubation period, we computed travel-time surfaces using a global motorized-travel friction surface map integrating road networks, land cover, rivers, topography, and transport infrastructure. Travel times were estimated from each affected health district (DRC) and ADM3 units (Uganda) to all destination countries in Africa. As armed conflict can substantially alter travel time, our estimates were adjusted for conflict-related disruptions using the most recent conflict data at ADM2 level obtained from the Armed Conflict Location & Event Data Project (ACLED), and calibrated against Google Maps driving-time estimates."
[^fanelli-2026a-methods-10]: [Fanelli et al. (2026a) "METHODS"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.07.21.26358330): "For land travel, the share of the population in each administrative unit travelling to each destination country was estimated through a gravity model calibrated on observed land mobility patterns."
[^fanelli-2026a-discussion-1]: [Fanelli et al. (2026a) "DISCUSSION"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.07.21.26358330): "Although uncertainty remains, sensitivity analyses reducing land mobility by 66% and 90% produced only limited changes in country rankings, supporting the robustness of our findings. Second, land-mediated importation risk was estimated assuming an average travel duration of 8h per day, and analyses were restricted to contiguous Africa because comparable data on maritime mobility are not available. Alternative travel durations produced no changes in the estimated geography of risk."
[^fanelli-2026a-methods-11]: [Fanelli et al. (2026a) "METHODS"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.07.21.26358330): "National epidemic readiness was assessed using several complementary frameworks, including the WHO State Parties Self-Assessment Annual Reporting (SPAR, 2025), the Global Health Security Index (GHS, 2021), the Infectious Disease Vulnerability Index (IDVI, 2016), and the Epidemic Risk Index (INFORM-WHO, 2020). From each framework, we extracted indicators describing *Healthcare system capacity* and *Emergency preparedness and response*."
[^fanelli-2026a-methods-12]: [Fanelli et al. (2026a) "METHODS"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.07.21.26358330): "Given its complete coverage of African countries, recent update, and direct relevance to the current WHO Framework for Health Emergency Preparedness and Response Capabilities for national public health agencies, SPAR was selected as the primary epidemic readiness metric for the main analysis (**Fig. 1fg**)."
[^fanelli-2026a-methods-13]: [Fanelli et al. (2026a) "METHODS"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.07.21.26358330): "Corresponding GHS indicators, complementary to SPAR indicators, were used in sensitivity analyses."
[^fanelli-2026a-methods-14]: [Fanelli et al. (2026a) "METHODS"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.07.21.26358330): "Amplification scenarios were designed to assess the potential impact of geographical expansion of the epidemic beyond the currently affected area into highly connected urban centers. Tested locations included the capital cities of the two affected countries, Kinshasa (DRC) and Kampala (Uganda), and the capitals of neighboring countries scoring highest in importation risk: Juba (South Sudan), Kigali (Rwanda), Bujumbura (Burundi), Nairobi (Kenya), and Lusaka (Zambia)."
[^fanelli-2026a-methods-15]: [Fanelli et al. (2026a) "METHODS"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.07.21.26358330): "To account for geographically heterogeneous reporting rates, we used the proportion of alerts investigated among reported alerts in each DRC province, averaged over the two weeks preceding 5 July 2026, relative to 100% reporting in Uganda. We explored alternative values in the sensitivity analysis."
[^fanelli-2026a-methods-21]: [Fanelli et al. (2026a) "METHODS"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.07.21.26358330): "We assumed that Ebola infected cases could travel only in their latent phase, as symptomatic patients would be expected to either remain in the affected country for clinical management or, if transferred internationally, travel under medically supervised and controlled conditions."
[^fanelli-2026a-discussion-3]: [Fanelli et al. (2026a) "DISCUSSION"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.07.21.26358330): "We also assumed homogeneous travel behaviour and exposure risk across travellers, although age, occupation, socioeconomic conditions, purpose of travel, and individual exposure may modify infection risk, as illustrated by the imported case in France involving a medical doctor working in the response."
[^fanelli-2026a-discussion-4]: [Fanelli et al. (2026a) "DISCUSSION"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.07.21.26358330): "Similarly, geographically heterogeneous under-ascertainment is difficult to quantify. We therefore used the proportion of investigated alerts as a proxy for reporting rates by health district, and found the results to be robust to alternative assumptions."
[^fanelli-2026a-discussion-5]: [Fanelli et al. (2026a) "DISCUSSION"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.07.21.26358330): "Second, land-mediated importation risk was estimated assuming an average travel duration of 8h per day, and analyses were restricted to contiguous Africa because comparable data on maritime mobility are not available. Alternative travel durations produced no changes in the estimated geography of risk."
[^fanelli-2026a-methods-22]: [Fanelli et al. (2026a) "METHODS"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.07.21.26358330): "Border restrictions and closures related to the current outbreak were not considered, because the implemented measures were partial, temporary and largely limited to official points of entry. [^fanelli-2026a-methods-23]: [Fanelli et al. (2026a) "METHODS"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.07.21.26358330): "Risks were weighted by the cumulative attack rate in each affected administrative unit."
[^fanelli-2026a-discussion-8]: [Fanelli et al. (2026a) "DISCUSSION"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.07.21.26358330): "Although uncertainty remains, sensitivity analyses reducing land mobility by 66% and 90% produced only limited changes in country rankings, supporting the robustness of our findings."
[^fanelli-2026a-discussion-9]: [Fanelli et al. (2026a) "DISCUSSION"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.07.21.26358330): "Second, land-mediated importation risk was estimated assuming an average travel duration of 8h per day, and analyses were restricted to contiguous Africa because comparable data on maritime mobility are not available."
[^fanelli-2026a-discussion-10]: [Fanelli et al. (2026a) "DISCUSSION"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.07.21.26358330): "Finally, we did not explicitly model behavioural adaptations or the impact of border control measures. Moreover, evidence from previous Ebola outbreaks highlights that travel restrictions have limited impact on spatial dissemination while potentially hampering response activities."
[^fanelli-2026a-methods-1]: [Fanelli et al. [^fanelli-2026a-methods-16]: [Fanelli et al. [^fanelli-2026a-methods-17]: [Fanelli et al. (2026a) "METHODS"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.07.21.26358330): "For Uganda, population estimates at administrative levels 0–3 were obtained from the United Nations Population Fund (UNFPA), based on Uganda Bureau of Statistics data."
[^fanelli-2026a-methods-18]: [Fanelli et al. [^fanelli-2026a-methods-19]: [Fanelli et al. [^fanelli-2026a-methods-20]: [Fanelli et al. [^verheyden-mudogo-2026a-abstract-2]: [Verheyden and Mudogo (2026a) "Abstract"](https://www.medrxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.08.12.26360244.full-text): "FOI had the highest top-10 hit rate (42.6%), approaching conventional significance against road-distance and no-connectivity comparators.
